\displaystyle+(T^{\prime})^{j-2}\Big(a(s)E^{\prime}_{j}(y,z,s)+T^{\prime}\cdot\big(G_{j+1}(y,z,s)+T^{\prime}G_{j+2}(y,z,s)+\cdots\big)\Big).

By choosing
m_{i}
greater than the vanishing order of
a(s)b(s)
along
H_{i}
for all
i=1,...,n
,
the function
a(s)b(s)
divides
T^{\prime}
.
As a result, the defining equation of
X
can be written as

\displaystyle F(x,y,z,s)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x^{2}+F_{2}(y,z,s)+\cdots+(T^{\prime})^{j-3}G_{j-1}

\displaystyle+(T^{\prime})^{j-2}a(s)\Big(E^{\prime}_{j}(y,z,s)+

\displaystyle b(s)E^{\prime}_{j+1}(y,z,s)+b(s)E^{\prime}_{j+2}(y,z,s)+\cdots\Big),

where each
E^{\prime}_{l}
is a holomorphic function, homogeneous in
(x,y,z)
of degree
l
.
In other words, after the blowups, we can write
F_{j}(y,z,s)=a^{\prime}(s)\cdot E^{\prime}_{j}(y,z,s)
,
where
a^{\prime}(s)=(T^{\prime})^{j-2}a(s)
.
Furthermore, the zero locus of
a^{\prime}(s)
is contained in
H
,
and
a^{\prime}(s)b(s)
divides

F_{j+1}+F_{j+2}+\cdots=a^{\prime}(s)b(s)E^{\prime}_{j+1}+a^{\prime}(s)b(s)E^{\prime}_{j+2}+\cdots.

In conclusion, by blowing up
X
and by denoting
a^{\prime}(s)
by
a(s)
,
we can make
a(s)b(s)
divides
F_{j+1}+\cdots
.
Equivalently, the defining function
F
becomes

\displaystyle F(x,y,z,s)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x^{2}+F_{2}(y,z,s)+\cdots+F_{j-1}(y,z,s)

\displaystyle+F_{j}(y,z,s)+F_{j+1}(y,z,s)+\cdots

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x^{2}+F_{2}(y,z,s)+\cdots+F_{j-1}(y,z,s)

\displaystyle+a(s)E_{j}(y,z,s)+a(s)b(s)R(y,z,s),

where
R(y,z,s)
is a holomorphic function with
R(y,z,s)=0\operatorname{mod}\,(y,z)^{j+1}
.

Remark 3.8
.

Assume that
F(x,y,z,s)=x^{2}+G(y,z,s)
with

G(y,z,s)=\sum_{i=0}^{3}R_{i}(y,z,s)\cdot y^{i}\cdot(q(s)z)^{3-i}+P(y,s),

where the zero locus of
q(s)
is contained in
H
,
and
P(y,s)=0\operatorname{mod}\,(y)^{4}
.
We blow up
X
at each
H_{i}
for
m_{i}
times,
and we choose
m_{i}
greater than the vanishing order of
q(s)
along
H_{i}
.
Particularly, there is a holomorphic function
b(s)
such that
T^{\prime}=q(s)b(s)
.
Up to shrinking
S
around the origin, we may assume that
b(s)
and
q(s)
are bounded on
S
.
The defining equation of
X
becomes

\displaystyle F(x,y,z,s)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x^{2}+(T^{\prime})^{-2}\cdot G(T^{\prime}y,T^{\prime}z,s)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x^{2}+(T^{\prime})^{-2}\cdot\Big(\sum_{i=0}^{3}R_{i}(T^{\prime}y,T^{\prime}z,s)\cdot(T^{\prime})^{3}y^{i}\cdot(q(s)z)^{3-i}+P(T^{\prime}y,s)\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x^{2}+T^{\prime}\cdot\sum_{i=0}^{3}R_{i}(T^{\prime}y,T^{\prime}z,s)\cdot y^{i}\cdot(q(s)z)^{3-i}+(T^{\prime})^{-2}\cdot P(T^{\prime}y,s).

We note that
P^{\prime}(y,s):=(T^{\prime})^{-2}\cdot P(T^{\prime}y,s)
is a holomorphic function in
(y,s)
.
We set
\zeta=q(s)z
and define

R_{i}^{\prime}(y,\zeta,s):=T^{\prime}R_{i}(T^{\prime}y,b(s)\cdot\zeta,s)=T^{\prime}R_{i}(T^{\prime}y,b(s)\cdot q(s)z,s)=T^{\prime}R_{i}(T^{\prime}y,T^{\prime}z,s),

for
i=0,2,3
.
Then each
R^{\prime}_{i}(y,\zeta,s)
is a holomorphic function on
\mathbb{D}^{2}\times S
, for
T^{\prime}
and
b(s)
are bounded on
S
.
Moreover, we have

F(x,y,z,s)=x^{2}+\sum_{i=0}^{3}R^{\prime}_{i}(y,\zeta,s)\cdot y^{i}\cdot\zeta^{3-i}+P^{\prime}(y,s),

which now can be viewed as a function in
(x,y,\zeta,s)
.
In conclusion, by blowing up
X
at the
H_{i}
’s,
we can make
F(x,y,z,s)
a holomorphic function
in
(x,y,\zeta,s)
.

4.
Modification to double-point singularities

Let
(S,H)
be a reduced snc pair, and let
o\in S
be a point.
We consider a holomorphic function
F
defined in a neighborhood of
(\mathbf{0}_{3},o)\in\mathbb{C}^{3}\times S
of the shape

F(x,y,z,s)=x^{2}+F_{2}(y,z,s)+F_{3}(y,z,s)+R(y,z,s),

where
(x,y,z)
are coordinates of
\mathbb{C}^{3}
and
s\in S
, the functions

F_{2}
and
F_{3}
are homogeneous polynomials in
(y,z)
, of degrees
2
and
3
respectively, with coefficients as holomorphic functions on
S
,
and
R(y,z,s)=0\operatorname{mod}\,(y,z)^{4}
.
We say that
F
is of
standard form
with respect to
(S,H)
if the following property holds.
Either
F_{2}(y,z,s)=a(s)\cdot G_{2}(y,z,s)
, where
G_{2}(y,z,s)
is a nonzero polynomials in
(y,z)
for any point
s\in S
, and the zero locus of
a(s)
is contained in
H
;
or
F_{2}(y,z,s)=0
and
F_{3}(y,z,s)=b(s)\cdot G_{3}(y,z,s)
, where
G_{3}(y,z,s)
is a nonzero polynomials in
(y,z)
for any point
s\in S
, and the zero locus of
b(s)
is contained in
H
.
The objective of this section is to prove the following proposition.

Proposition 4.1
.

Let
X
be a complex analytic variety.
Let
S
be a codimension 2 irreducible component of
X_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sing}}}
.
Assume that there is a proper Zariski closed subset
C\subseteq S
such that

X
has the same type of canonical singularities at points of
S\setminus C
.
Then there is a projective bimeromorphic morphism

f\colon Y\rightarrow X

such that the following properties hold.

(1)

f
is the composition of blowups with centers strictly contained in
S
.

(2)

If
S_{Y}
is the strict transform of
S
in
Y
, then
(S_{Y},H_{Y})
is a snc pair, where
H_{Y}
is the reduced divisor,
whose support is the union of the preimage of
C
in
S_{Y}
and the intersection of
S_{Y}
with the
f
-exceptional locus.

(3)

For every point
o\in S_{Y}
, there is a neighborhood
U\subseteq Y
of
o
, such that

U
is isomorphic to a hypersurface in
\mathbb{D}^{3}\times(S_{Y}\cap U)
, defined by an equation of standard form with respect to
(S_{Y}\cap U,H_{Y}\cap U)
.

(4)

Y
has the same type of canonical singularities at points of
S_{Y}\setminus H_{Y}
.

(5)

The construction of
f
is functorial.

We remark that the items (1)-(4) still hold if we blow up
Y
at a stratum of
H_{Y}
.
The proof of the proposition is divided into three steps.
In the first one, we reduce to the case when
X
has hypersurface singularities.
In the second step, we reduce to the case when
X
has double-point singularities.
We arrange the defining equation into standard form in the last step.

4.A.
Hypersurface singularities

In this subsection, we consider the following situation.
Let
X
be a complex analytic variety and let
S\subseteq X
be a closed subspace.
Assume that
S
is irreducible and smooth of dimension
n
.
Let
W=\mathbb{D}^{N}\times S
.
Assume that
X
is isomorphic to the closed subspace in
W
defined by
holomorphic functions
f_{j}(x_{1},...,x_{N},s)
, where
(x_{1},...,x_{N})
are the coordinates of
\mathbb{C}^{N}
,
s\in S
is a point, and
j=1,...,k
for some positive integer
k
.
Assume that the composite inclusion
S\subseteq X\subseteq W
coincides with
\{\mathbf{0}_{N}\}\times S\subseteq W
.
In particular,
f_{j}(\mathbf{0}_{N},s)=0
for all
s\in S
and for all
j=1,...,k
.
We set

\mathrm{d}_{x}f_{j}:=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{\partial f_{j}}{\partial x_{i}}\mathrm{d}x_{i}

and we let
r(s)
be the rank of
(\mathrm{d}_{x}f_{j}(\mathbf{0}_{N},s))_{j=1,...,k}
, in the vector space generated by
\mathrm{d}x_{1},...,\mathrm{d}x_{N}
.
We remark that such an embedding
X\subseteq W
always exists locally around every point of
S
, as
S
is smooth.

Lemma 4.2
.

With the notation above, the embedding dimension of
X
at a point
s\in S\subseteq X
is equal to
N+n-r(s)
.

Proof.

Since
S
is smooth, by Jacobi criterion (see for example
[
GR84
, Section 6.1]
),
the embedding dimension of
X
is equal to
N+n-R(s)
,
where
R(s)
is the rank of
(\mathrm{d}f_{j}(\mathbf{0}_{N},s))_{j=1,...,k}
, in the vector space
\Omega^{1}_{W,(\mathbf{0}_{N},s)}
.
Since
f_{j}(\mathbf{0}_{N},s)=0
for all
s\in S
and for all
j=1,...,k
,
the partial derivatives of
f_{j}
, with respect to the variables of
S
,
are all zero along
\{\mathbf{0}_{N}\}\times S
, for all
j=1,...,k
.
It follows that
R(s)
is equal to
r(s)
.
This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Remark 4.3
.

With the notation above,
we remark that
\mathrm{d}_{x}f_{j}(\mathbf{0}_{N},s)
represents the linear part of
f_{j}
in
(x_{1},...,x_{N})
.
Indeed, such a linear part is equal to

\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{\partial f_{j}}{\partial x_{i}}(\mathbf{0}_{N},s)\cdot x_{i}.

Lemma 4.4
.

With the notation above, let
r=\operatorname{min}\{r(s)\ |\ s\in S\}
.
In other words,
N+n-r
is the maximum of the embedding dimensions of
X
at the points of
S
.
Assume that
X
has embedding dimension smaller than
N+n-r
at general points of
S
.
Then there is a projective bimeromorphic morphism
f\colon Y\rightarrow X
obtained by blowing up
X
at ideal sheaves whose cosupports are strictly contained in
S
,
such that the maximum of the embedding dimensions of
Y
at the points of
S_{Y}
is at most
N+n-r-1
, where
S_{Y}
is the strict transform of
S
in
Y
.
Moreover,
S_{Y}
is smooth and the construction of

f
is functorial, which depends only on the isomorphism class of
X
around
S
.
In particular, it is independent of
N
.

Proof.

Let
o\in S
be a point at which the embedding dimension of
X
is
N+n-r
.
We will first work locally around
o
.
In particular, we are allowed to shrink
S
around
o
.
Then, by showing that the operation in the lemma is functorial, we can glue the local constructions in the global setting.

Up to a linear change of the coordinates
(x_{1},...,x_{N})
of
\mathbb{C}^{N}
,
and up to permuting the functions
f_{j}
,
we may assume that

\mathrm{d}_{x}f_{j}(\mathbf{0}_{N},o)=\mathrm{d}x_{j}

for
j=1,...,r.

Then, by subtracting each
f_{j}
, with
j>r
, some linear combination, with coefficients as holomorphic functions on
S
, of the
f_{j}
’s with
j=1,...,r
, we may assume that

\frac{\partial f_{j}}{\partial x_{i}}(\mathbf{0}_{N},s)=0

for
i=1,...,r
,
j>r
and all
s\in S
contained in a neighborhood of
o
.
In particular, the matrix
\Big[\frac{\partial f_{j}}{\partial x_{i}}(\mathbf{0}_{N},s)\Big]_{1\leq i\leq N,1\leq j\leq k}
, whose entries are holomorphic functions on
S
, is of the shape

\begin{bmatrix}A(s)&\mathbf{0}\\
*&B(s)&\end{bmatrix},

where
A(s)
is a
r\times r
invertible matrix with
A(o)=\mathrm{Id}_{r}
.
Let
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathcal{O}(S)
be the ideal generated by

\frac{\partial f_{j}}{\partial x_{i}}(\mathbf{0}_{N},s)\mbox{ for all }i,j>r,

equivalently, the entries of the matrix
B
above.
Then, up to shrinking
S
, the closed subset contained in
S
, at which
X
has embedding dimension
N+n-r
, is the cosupport of
\mathcal{I}
.
By assumption, this cosupport is a proper subset of
S
.

Let
h\colon Z\rightarrow X
be the projective bimeromorphic morphism induced by blowing up the ideal
\mathcal{I}
on
S
, see Subsection
3.B
.
That is,
h
is the blowup of
X
at the ideal
(x_{1},...,x_{N},\mathcal{I})
.
Let
S_{Z}
be the strict transform of
S
in
Z
and
o_{Z}\in S_{Z}
a point lying over
o
.
Then there is a neighborhood
U
of
o_{Z}
in
Z
, which can be viewed as a closed subset of

\mathbb{D}^{N}\times(U\cap S_{Z}).

Moreover,
h
can be written in coordinates as

h\colon(x_{1}^{\prime},...,x_{N}^{\prime},s^{\prime})\mapstochar\rightarrow(tx_{1}^{\prime},...,tx_{N}^{\prime},h(s^{\prime})),

where
t
is a holomorphic function on
U\cap S_{Z}
which defines the exceptional divisor of
S_{Z}\rightarrow S
on
U\cap S_{Z}
.
We define

f^{\prime}_{j}(x_{1}^{\prime},...,x_{N}^{\prime},s^{\prime})=\frac{1}{t}(h^{*}f_{j})(x_{1},...,x_{N},s)=\frac{1}{t}f_{j}(tx_{1}^{\prime},...,tx_{N}^{\prime},h(s^{\prime}))

for
j=1,...,r
, and

f^{\prime}_{j}(x_{1}^{\prime},...,x_{N}^{\prime},s^{\prime})=\frac{1}{t^{2}}(h^{*}f_{j})(x_{1},...,x_{N},s)=\frac{1}{t^{2}}f_{j}(tx_{1}^{\prime},...,tx_{N}^{\prime},h(s^{\prime}))

for
j=r+1,...,k
.
Then as shown in Subsection
3.B
,
all of the
f^{\prime}_{j}
are holomorphic functions and vanish on
Z
.
We see that

\mathrm{d}_{x^{\prime}}f^{\prime}_{j}(\mathbf{0}_{N},o_{Z})=\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}_{j}

for
j=1,...,r.

Furthermore, since the ideal
\mathcal{I}
is generated by
\frac{\partial f_{j}}{\partial x_{i}}(\mathbf{0}_{N},s)
, where
i,j>r
,
there are some
i,j>r
such that

\frac{\partial f^{\prime}_{j}}{\partial x^{\prime}_{i}}(\mathbf{0}_{N},o_{Z})\neq 0.

As a consequence, after the blowup, the rank of
(\mathrm{d}_{x^{\prime}}f^{\prime}_{j}(\mathbf{0}_{N},o_{Z}))_{j=1,...,k}
is at least
r(o)+1
.
In particular, if
S_{Z}
is smooth at
o_{Z}
, then the embedding dimension of
Z
at
o_{Z}
is at most
N+n-r-1
.

Let
f\colon Y\rightarrow X
be the projective bimeromorphic morphism induced by the functorial principalization of
(g|_{S_{Z}})^{-1}\mathcal{I}\cdot\mathcal{O}_{S_{Z}}
on
S_{Z}
.
In particular, the strict transform
S_{Y}
of
S
in
Y
is smooth.
Since
Y\rightarrow Z
is the composition of blowups with centers strictly contained in
S_{Z}
,
the similar computation as above shows that

Y
has embedding dimension at most
N+n-r-1
at a point
o_{Y}\in S_{Y}
lying over
o
.

It remains to show that the construction is functorial.
This is equivalent to show that, around
o\in S
,
the ideal
\mathcal{I}
is independent of the choice of coordinates.
We first note that, in the construction above, the ideal
\mathcal{I}
is also the ideal generated by all determinants of
(r+1)
-minors of the matrix

\Big[\frac{\partial f_{j}}{\partial x_{i}}(\mathbf{0}_{N},s)\Big]_{1\leq i\leq N,1\leq j\leq k}.

Therefore, if
(g_{e})_{e=1,...,t}
is another collection of functions defining
X
, then they define the same ideal
\mathcal{I}
.
Moreover, we assume that
\psi\colon(x^{\prime}_{1},...,x^{\prime}_{N},s)\rightarrow(x_{1},...,x_{N},s)
is a change of coordinates of
\mathbb{C}^{N}\times S
.
Then the
N\times N
matrix

\Big[\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{\prime}_{i}}x_{l}(\mathbf{0}_{N},s)\Big]_{1\leq i\leq N,1\leq l\leq N}

is invertible around
o\in S
.
Hence, by the chain rule, we deduce that the functions
f_{j}\circ\psi
, which define
X
inside
\mathbb{C}^{N}\times S
with the coordinates system
(x^{\prime}_{1},...,x^{\prime}_{N},s)
,
determine the same ideal
\mathcal{I}
.

Next we will reduce to the case when
r=0
, that is, the case when
N+n
is the embedding dimension of
X
at
o
.
Indeed, from the previous discussions, thanks to the implicit function theorem,
up to a change of coordinate of
\mathbb{C}^{N}\times S
,
we may assume that
(f_{1},...,f_{r})=(x_{1},...,x_{r})
.
For any
j=r+1,...,k
, we set

g_{j}(x_{r+1},...,x_{N})=f_{j}(0,...,0,x_{r+1},...,x_{N}).

It follows that there is a closed embedding
X\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{N-r}\times S
,
such that, with the coordinate system
(x_{r+1},...,x_{N},s)
, the subspace
X
is defined by
g_{r+1},...,g_{k}
.
The ideal sheaf
\mathcal{I}
is defined by the entries of the matrix

\Big[\frac{\partial g_{j}}{\partial x_{i}}\Big]_{r+1\leq i\leq N,r+1\leq j\leq k}.

Therefore, we may always assume that
r=0
and that
N+n
is the embedding dimension of
X
at
o
,
for the construction of
\mathcal{I}
around
o
.
Assume that there is another coordinates system
(x_{1}^{\prime},...,x_{N}^{\prime},s^{\prime})
of
\mathbb{C}^{N}\times S
,
such that the subspace
X\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{N}\times S
is defined by a family
(g_{e}(x_{1}^{\prime},...,x_{N}^{\prime},s^{\prime}))_{e=1,...,t}
of functions.
Then, locally around
o
, there is a holomorphic map

\varphi\colon(x_{1},...,x_{N},s)\mapstochar\rightarrow(x_{1}^{\prime},...,x_{N}^{\prime},s^{\prime}),

where we regard
s^{\prime}
and
x_{1}^{\prime},...,x_{N}^{\prime}
as holomorphic functions in
(x_{1},...,x_{N},s)
.
We note that
s^{\prime}(\mathbf{0}_{N},s)=s
for any
s\in S
.
We define the following
N\times N
matrix

\Theta(s)=\Big[\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}}x_{l}^{\prime}(\mathbf{0}_{N},s)\Big]_{1\leq i\leq N,1\leq l\leq N}.

As shown in Lemma
4.2
, the partial derivatives of
g_{e}
with respect to the variables of
S
are all 0 on
\{\mathbf{0}_{N}\}\times S
.
Hence for any
s\in S
, we have

\Big[\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}}(g_{e}\circ\varphi)(\mathbf{0}_{N},s)\Big]_{1\leq i\leq N,1\leq e\leq t}=\Theta(s)\cdot\Big[(\frac{\partial g_{e}}{\partial x^{\prime}_{l}}(\mathbf{0}_{N},s^{\prime}(\mathbf{0}_{N},s))\Big]_{1\leq l\leq N,1\leq e\leq t}

As we have assume that
r=0
and that
N+n
is the embedding dimension of
X
at
o
,
by considering the entries in the both sides above,
we deduce that the ideal
\mathcal{I}
constructed
via

(g_{e}\circ\varphi)_{e=1,...,t}
on the coordinates system
(x_{1},...,x_{N},s)
is contained in the one constructed
via

(g_{e})_{e=1,...,t}
on the coordinates system
(x_{1}^{\prime},...,x_{N}^{\prime},s^{\prime})
.
Since
(g_{e}\circ\varphi)_{e=1,...,t}
also defines the inclusion
X\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{N}\times S
with respect to the coordinates system
(x_{1},...,x_{N},s)
,
it follows that the ideal constructed
via

(f_{j})_{j=1,...,k}
on the coordinates system
(x_{1},...,x_{N},s)
is contained in the one constructed
via

(g_{e})_{e=1,...,t}
on the coordinates system
(x_{1}^{\prime},...,x_{N}^{\prime},s^{\prime})
.
Since the roles of the two coordinates systems are symmetric,
we conclude that the ideal
\mathcal{I}
we have constructed is independent on the choice of coordinates.
This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Example 4.5
.

Assume that
S=\mathbb{C}^{2}
with coordinates
(a,b)
, and that
X\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{4}\times S
is defined by the ideal generated by the following functions,

\displaystyle f_{1}(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4},a,b)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x_{3}+x_{3}^{2}

\displaystyle f_{2}(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4},a,b)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x_{3}+ax_{1}+bx_{1}x_{2}

\displaystyle f_{3}(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4},a,b)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x_{3}+bx_{1}+x_{2}^{2}+x_{4}^{3}.

Then when
a\neq 0
or
b\neq 0
, and
x_{1}=x_{2}=x_{3}=x_{4}=0
,
the rank of the Jacobi matrix of
(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3})
is 2.
At
\mathbf{0}_{6}
, the rank of the Jacobi matrix is 1.
Then
X
has embedding dimension 5 at this point.
Now we blow up
\mathbb{C}^{6}
at the ideal
(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4},a,b)
and obtain
h\colon W\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{6}
.
Then on the open set
U=W\setminus h^{-1}_{*}(\{a=0\})
,
h
can be written in coordinate as

h\colon(x_{1}^{\prime},x_{2}^{\prime},x_{3}^{\prime},x^{\prime}_{4},\alpha,\beta)\mapstochar\rightarrow(\alpha x_{1}^{\prime},\alpha x_{2}^{\prime},\alpha x_{3}^{\prime},\alpha x^{\prime}_{4},\alpha,\alpha\beta).

Then we have

\displaystyle h^{*}f_{1}(x^{\prime}_{1},x^{\prime}_{2},x^{\prime}_{3},x^{\prime}_{4},\alpha,\beta)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\alpha(x^{\prime}_{3}+\alpha(x^{\prime}_{3})^{2})

\displaystyle h^{*}f_{2}(x^{\prime}_{1},x^{\prime}_{2},x^{\prime}_{3},x^{\prime}_{4},\alpha,\beta)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\alpha(x^{\prime}_{3}+\alpha x^{\prime}_{1}+\alpha^{2}\beta x^{\prime}_{1}x^{\prime}_{2})

\displaystyle h^{*}f_{3}(x^{\prime}_{1},x^{\prime}_{2},x^{\prime}_{3},x^{\prime}_{4},\alpha,\beta)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\alpha(x^{\prime}_{3}+\alpha\beta x^{\prime}_{1}+\alpha(x^{\prime}_{2})^{2}+\alpha^{2}(x^{\prime}_{4})^{3}).

We set
g_{j}(x^{\prime}_{1},x^{\prime}_{2},x^{\prime}_{3},x^{\prime}_{4},\alpha,\beta)=\frac{1}{\alpha}h^{*}f_{j}(x^{\prime}_{1},x^{\prime}_{2},x^{\prime}_{3},x^{\prime}_{4},\alpha,\beta)

for
j=1,2,3
.
We notice that
g_{2}-g_{1}
and
g_{3}-g_{1}
are divisible by
\alpha
.
We set

p_{2}=\frac{1}{\alpha}(g_{2}-g_{1})\mbox{ and }p_{3}=\frac{1}{\alpha}(g_{3}-g_{1}).

Then the strict transform
X^{\prime}
of
X
in
W
is defined, on
U
, by the ideal generated by the following functions

\displaystyle g_{1}(x^{\prime}_{1},x^{\prime}_{2},x^{\prime}_{3},x^{\prime}_{4},\alpha,\beta)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x^{\prime}_{3}+\alpha(x^{\prime}_{3})^{2}

\displaystyle p_{2}(x^{\prime}_{1},x^{\prime}_{2},x^{\prime}_{3},x^{\prime}_{4},\alpha,\beta)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x^{\prime}_{1}+\alpha\beta x^{\prime}_{1}x^{\prime}_{2}-(x^{\prime}_{3})^{2}

\displaystyle p_{3}(x^{\prime}_{1},x^{\prime}_{2},x^{\prime}_{3},x^{\prime}_{4},\alpha,\beta)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta x^{\prime}_{1}+(x^{\prime}_{2})^{2}+\alpha(x^{\prime}_{4})^{3}-(x^{\prime}_{3})^{2}.

Then the rank of the Jacobi matrix of
(g_{1},p_{1},p_{2})
is at least 2 on
U
.
We can perform the same calculation on
U^{\prime}=W\setminus h^{-1}_{*}(\{b=0\})
.
In addition, the strict transform
S^{\prime}
of
S
in
W
is contained in
U\cup U^{\prime}
.
This shows that
X^{\prime}
has embedding dimension at most 4 around
S^{\prime}
.

Corollary 4.6
.

With the notation in Lemma
4.4
, we assume that
X
has hypersurface singularities (respectively is smooth) around general points of
S
.
Then there is a projective bimeromorphic morphism
f\colon X^{\prime}\rightarrow X
such that the following properties hold.

(1)

f
is obtained by a sequence of blowups at centers strictly contained in
S
.

(2)

The strict transform
S^{\prime}
of
S
in
X^{\prime}
is smooth.

(3)

X^{\prime}
has hypersurface singularities (respectively is smooth) around
S^{\prime}
.

(4)

The construction of
f
is functorial.

Proof.

We will only treat the case when
X
has hypersurface singularities around general points of
S
, the other case can be proved by the same argument.
Assume that the maximum of embedding dimensions of
X
at points of
S
is
\operatorname{dim}X+r
.
If
r=1
, then
X
has hypersurface singularities around
S
.
Otherwise, there is a sequence
of projective bimeromorphic morphisms

X_{e}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow X_{1}=X,

such that
each
X_{i}\rightarrow X_{i+1}
is the functorial operation in Lemma
4.4
,
and that the maximum of embedding dimensions of
X_{e}
at points of
S_{e}
is
\operatorname{dim}X+1
,
where
S_{e}
is the strict transform of
S
in
X_{e}
.
We let
X^{\prime}=X_{e}
and let
f\colon X^{\prime}\rightarrow X
be the composition of the sequence.
Then it satisfies all the properties of the corollary.
∎

4.B.
Double-point singularities

We will prove the following statement in this subsection.

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
X
be a complex analytic variety of dimension
n
and let
S\subseteq X_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sing}}}
be an irreducible component of codimension
2
.
Assume that
S
is smooth,
that
X
has hypersurface singularities around
S
,
and that
X
has double-point singularities around general points of
S
.
Then there is a projective bimeromorphic morphism
f\colon X^{\prime}\rightarrow X
such that the following properties hold.

(1)

f
is obtained by a sequence of blowups at centers strictly contained in
S
.

(2)

The strict transform
S^{\prime}
of
S
in
X^{\prime}
is smooth.

(3)

X^{\prime}
has double-point singularities around
S^{\prime}
.

(4)

The construction of
f
is functorial.

Proof.

It is enough to study the problem locally.
Indeed, once we prove the functoriality, we can glue our local constructions in the global setting.
Let
o\in S\subseteq X
be a point.
We may assume that
S
is a polydisc in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
and
o\in S
is the origin.
Then locally around
o
,
we may assume that
X
is the hypersurface in
\mathbb{D}^{3}\times S
, defined by the analytic function

F(x,y,z,s)=\sum_{i\geq 0}F_{i}(x,y,z,s),

where
(x,y,z)
are the coordinates of
\mathbb{D}^{3}
,
s\in S
, and
F_{i}
is a homogeneous polynomial in
(x,y,z)
of degree
i
with coefficients as holomorphic functions on
S
.
Furthermore, the composite inclusion
S\subseteq X\subseteq\mathbb{D}^{3}\times S
coincides with
\{\mathbf{0}_{3}\}\times S\subseteq\mathbb{D}^{3}\times S
.
In particular, we have
F_{0}=0
.
Since
S\subseteq X_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sing}}}
, it follows that
F_{1}=0
.
Since
X
has double-point singularities around general points of
S
,
we have
F_{2}\neq 0
.

Let
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathcal{O}(S)
be the ideal generated by the coefficients of
F_{2}
.
If
D\subseteq S
is the closed subset defined by
\mathcal{I}
,
then at every point in
S\setminus D
,

X
has double-point singularities.

Let
h\colon X^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow X
be the blowup up of
X
at the ideal
(x,y,z,\mathcal{I})
.
As shown in Subsection
3.B
, at every point
o^{\prime\prime}
in the strict transform
S^{\prime\prime}
of
S
in
X^{\prime\prime}
, lying over
o
,
there is an open neighborhood
U^{\prime\prime}\subseteq X^{\prime\prime}
of
o^{\prime\prime}
such that

U^{\prime\prime}
is isomorphic to the hypersurface in
\mathbb{D}^{3}\times(S^{\prime\prime}\cap U^{\prime\prime})
defined by the holomorphic function

G(x^{\prime\prime},y^{\prime\prime},z^{\prime\prime},s^{\prime\prime})=G_{2}(x^{\prime\prime},y^{\prime\prime},z^{\prime\prime},s^{\prime\prime})+R(x^{\prime\prime},y^{\prime\prime},z^{\prime\prime},s^{\prime\prime})

where
G_{2}
is a homogeneous quadratic polynomial in
(x^{\prime\prime},y^{\prime\prime},z^{\prime\prime})
with coefficients as holomorphic functions on
S^{\prime\prime}
, and the terms of
R
have degrees at least
3
in
(x^{\prime\prime},y^{\prime\prime},z^{\prime\prime})
.
Furthermore,
G_{2}(x^{\prime\prime},y^{\prime\prime},z^{\prime\prime},o^{\prime\prime})
is a nonzero polynomial in
(x^{\prime\prime},y^{\prime\prime},z^{\prime\prime})
.
In particular, if
S^{\prime\prime}
is smooth at
o^{\prime\prime}
, then
X^{\prime\prime}
has double-point singularities at
o^{\prime\prime}
.

Let
X^{\prime}\rightarrow X^{\prime\prime}
be the projective bimeromorphic morphism induced by the functorial principalization of the ideal
(h|_{S^{\prime\prime}})^{-1}\mathcal{I}\cdot\mathcal{O}_{S^{\prime\prime}}
on
S^{\prime\prime}
, see Subsection
3.B
.
In particular, the strict transform
S^{\prime}
of
S
in
X^{\prime}
is smooth.
Since
X^{\prime}\rightarrow X^{\prime\prime}
is the composition of blowups with center strictly contained in
S^{\prime\prime}
,
the computations of Subsection
3.B
implies that

X^{\prime}
has double-point singularities along
S^{\prime}
.

To prove that construction is functorial,
it is enough to show that the ideal
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathcal{O}(S)

is independent of the choice of coordinates.
Let
(\overline{x},\overline{y},\overline{z},\overline{s})
be some other choice of coordinates of
\mathbb{D}^{3}\times S

such that
X
is defined by
\overline{F}(\overline{x},\overline{y},\overline{z},\overline{s})=0
.
Let
\varphi\colon(x,y,z,s)\mapstochar\rightarrow(\overline{x},\overline{y},\overline{z},\overline{s})
be the change of coordinates,
and we regard
\overline{x},\overline{y},\overline{z},\overline{s}
as vector-valued holomorphic functions in
(x,y,z,s)
.
Then we have

\overline{s}(0,0,0,s)=s

for all
s\in S
.
Hence
\overline{s}(x,y,z,s)=s\operatorname{mod}\,(x,y,z)
.
There is a
3\times 3
matrix
\Theta(s)
,
whose entries are holomorphic functions on
S
, such that

(\overline{x},\overline{y},\overline{z})=(x,y,z)\cdot\Theta(s)\operatorname{mod}\,(x,y,z)^{2}.

We decompose
\overline{F}=\sum_{i\geq 2}\overline{F}_{i}
into the sum of its homogeneous parts in
(\overline{x},\overline{y},\overline{z})
.
Then we have

\displaystyle(\varphi^{*}\overline{F})(x,y,z,s)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\overline{F}(\overline{x},\overline{y},\overline{z},\overline{s})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\overline{F}_{2}((x,y,z)\cdot\Theta(s),s)\operatorname{mod}\,(x,y,z)^{3}.

Since both the equations
\varphi^{*}\overline{F}=0
and
F=0
define the hypersurface
X
,
there is a unit function
U(x,y,z,s)
such that

F=U\cdot\varphi^{*}\overline{F}.

We deduce that
the ideal
\mathcal{I}
generated by the coefficients of
F_{2}(x,y,z,s)

is the same as the one generated by the coefficients of
\overline{F}_{2}((x,y,z)\cdot\Theta(s),s)
.
This implies that
\mathcal{I}
is contained in the ideal generated by the coefficients of
\overline{F}_{2}(\overline{x},\overline{y},\overline{z},\overline{s})
.
Since the roles of the coordinates systems are symmetric,
we deduce that the ideal
\mathcal{I}
we have constructed is independent of the choice of coordinates.
This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

We have the following observation for double-point singularities.

Lemma 4.8
.

Let
X
be a complex analytic variety and let
S\subseteq X_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sing}}}
be an irreducible component of codimension at least
2
.
Assume that
X
has double-point singularities around
S
.
Then there is an open neighborhood
U
of
S
such that
U
is locally irreducible.
Equivalently, for every point
x\in U
, the stalk
\mathcal{O}_{X,x}
is an integral domain.

Proof.

Let
f\colon Y\rightarrow X
be the normalization of
X
, and let
x\in X
be a point.
If
\mathcal{O}_{X,x}
is an integral domain, then
f
is a homeomorphism over an open neighborhood of
x
by
[
GR84
, Corollary on page 163]
.
Conversely, if
\mathcal{O}_{X,x}
is not an integral domain, then there is an open neighborhood of
x
which is not irreducible by the local decomposition lemma (see
[
GR84
, page 79]
).
In particular,
f
is not a homeomorphism over
x
.

Let
s\in S
be a point.
We claim that
\mathcal{O}_{X,s}
is an integral domain.
Assume the contrary.
Then there is an open neighborhood
Z
of
s
in
X
such that
Z
is not irreducible.
We may assume that
Z\subseteq\mathbb{D}^{n+1}
, and that
Z
is a double cover of
\mathbb{D}^{n}
, where
n
is the dimension of
X
.
Then
Z
has two irreducible components
Z_{1}
,
Z_{2}
, and each of them is a smooth divisor in
\mathbb{D}^{n+1}
.
It follows that
Z_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sing}}}=Z_{1}\cap Z_{2}
, which is pure of dimension
n-1
.
However, an irreducible component of
S\cap Z
is an irreducible component of
Z_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sing}}}
, and is of dimension at most
n-2
.
This is a contradiction.

As a consequence, for every point
s\in S
, there is an open neighborhood
V_{s}
of
s
in
X
, such that the normalization morphism
f
is an homeomorphism over
V_{s}
.
It follows that
V_{s}
is locally irreducible.
In the end, we let
U=\bigcup_{s\in S}V_{s}
.
Then
U
is a locally irreducible neighborhood of
S
.
This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

4.C.
Equations of standard form

We will complete the proof of Proposition
4.1
in this subsection.

Lemma 4.9
.

Let
S
be a polydisc in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
.
Let
X\subseteq\mathbb{D}^{3}\times S
be the hypersurface defined by the equation

F(x,y,z,s)=x^{2}+F_{2}(y,z,s)+F_{3}(y,z,s)+R(y,z,s)=0,

where
(x,y,z)
are the coordinates of
\mathbb{D}^{3}
and
s\in S
.
Moreover, for
i=2,3
,
F_{i}(y,z,s)
is a homogeneous polynomial of degree
i
in
(y,z)
, with coefficients as holomorphic functions on
S
.
The function
R(y,z,s)
is holomorphic and its terms have degree at least 4 in
(y,z)
.
We assume that either
F_{2}
or
F_{3}
is not identically zero,
and define an ideal
\mathcal{I}
of
\mathcal{O}(S)

as follows.
If
F_{2}\neq 0
, then
\mathcal{I}
is the ideal generated by the coefficients of
F_{2}
.
Otherwise
\mathcal{I}
is the ideal generated by the coefficients of
F_{3}
.
Then the property that
F_{2}\neq 0
, and the ideal
\mathcal{I}
, depend only on the isomorphism classes of
X
,
and is independent of the choice of coordinates
(x,y,z,s)
.

Proof.

Assume that we have another choice of coordinates
(a,b,c,s^{\prime})
such that
X
is defined as

G(a,b,c,s^{\prime})=a^{2}+G_{2}(b,c,s^{\prime})+G_{3}(b,c,s^{\prime})+P(b,c,s^{\prime})=0.

We denote the change of coordinates by

\varphi\colon(x,y,z,s)\mapstochar\rightarrow(a,b,c,s^{\prime}),

and we can regards
a,b,c,s^{\prime}
as vector-valued holomorphic functions in
(x,y,z,s)
.
Then
s^{\prime}(0,0,0,s)=s
for all
s\in S
.
Thus
s^{\prime}(x,y,z,s)=s\operatorname{mod}\,(x,y,z)
.
If we set
(\varphi^{*}G)(x,y,z,s)=G(a,b,c,s^{\prime})
, then

(4.1)

(\varphi^{*}G)(x,y,z,s)=(\mathrm{unit})\cdot F(x,y,z,s).

First we assume that
G_{2}=0
.
Then, by comparing the coefficient before the term
x^{2}
in (
4.1
),
we can write

a(x,y,z,s)=l(x,y,z,s)\operatorname{mod}\,(y,z)^{2},

where
l=(\mathrm{unit})\cdot x+l_{1}(y,z,s)
, and
l_{1}(y,z,s)
is linear in
(y,z)
.
Then we have

(\varphi^{*}G)(x,y,z,s)=l(x,y,z,s)^{2}\operatorname{mod}\,(x,y,z)^{3}.

Thus, up to a nonzero multiplicative constant,
the term
x\cdot l_{1}(y,z,s)
belongs to

(\varphi^{*}G)(x,y,z,s)\operatorname{mod}\,(x,y,z)^{3}.

Comparing with (
4.1
), we deduce that
l_{1}(y,z,s)=0
.
Hence
l(x,y,z,s)=(\mathrm{unit})\cdot x
and
F_{2}=0
.

We note that

(4.2)

(\mathrm{unit})\cdot F(x,y,z,s)=u(s)\cdot F_{3}(y,z,s)\operatorname{mod}\,(x,(y,z)^{4}),

where
u(s)
is a unit.
We write
b(x,y,z,s)=b_{1}(y,z,s)+b_{1}^{\prime}(s)x+b_{>1}(x,y,z,s)
, where
b_{1}
is linear in
(y,z)
and
b_{>1}(x,y,z,s)=0\operatorname{mod}\,(x,y,z)^{2}
.
Similarly, we write
c(x,y,z,s)=c_{1}(y,z,s)+c_{1}^{\prime}(s)x+c_{>1}(x,y,z,s)
.
Then

(4.3)

\begin{split}(\varphi^{*}G)(x,y,z,s)&=G(a,b,c,s^{\prime})\\
&=a^{2}+G_{3}(b,c,s^{\prime})+P(b,c,s^{\prime})\\
&=G_{3}(b_{1}(y,z,s),c_{1}(y,z,s),s)\operatorname{mod}\,(x,(y,z)^{4}).\end{split}

Here, for the notation
G_{3}(b_{1}(y,z,s),c_{1}(y,z,s),s)
, if

G_{3}(b,c,s^{\prime})=\sum_{i=1}^{3}\alpha_{i}(s^{\prime})b^{i}c^{3-i},

then

G_{3}(b_{1}(y,z,s),c_{1}(y,z,s),s)=\sum_{i=1}^{3}\alpha_{i}(s)b_{1}(y,z,s)^{i}c(y,z,s)^{3-i}.

This is well-defined since the functions
\alpha_{i}
are functions on
S
.
By comparing (
4.3
) with (
4.1
) and (
4.2
),
we deduce that

G_{3}(b_{1}(y,z,s),c_{1}(y,z,s),s)=u(s)\cdot F_{3}(y,z,s).

As a consequence, the ideal generated by the coefficients of
F_{3}(y,z,s)
, regarded as a homogeneous polynomials in
(y,z)
, is contained in the ideal generated by the coefficients of
G_{3}(b,c,s^{\prime})
.
Hence, by symmetry, the ideal
\mathcal{I}
we have constructed is independent of the choice of coordinates in this case.

Next we assume that
G_{2}\neq 0
.
In this case, we have

(4.4)

(\mathrm{unit})\cdot F(x,y,z,s)=u(s)\cdot(x^{2}+F_{2}(y,z,s))\operatorname{mod}\,(x,y,z)^{3},

where
u(s)
is a unit.
There is a
3\times 3
matrix
\Theta(s)
, with coefficients as holomorphic functions in
S
such that

(a,b,c)=(x,y,z)\cdot\Theta(s)\operatorname{mod}\,(x,y,z)^{2}.

Let
(a_{1},b_{1},c_{1})=(x,y,z)\cdot\Theta(s)
.
Then we have

(\varphi^{*}G)(x,y,z,s)=a_{1}^{2}+G_{2}(b_{1},c_{1},s)\operatorname{mod}\,(x,y,z)^{3}.

Comparing the previous equation with (
4.1
) and
(
4.4
)
,
we deduce that

a_{1}^{2}+G_{2}(b_{1},c_{1},s)=u(s)(x^{2}+F_{2}(y,z,s)).

Both sides of the equation above can be viewed as quadratic forms in three variables, with coefficients as holomorphic functions on
S
.
We denote the LHS by
L(a_{1},b_{1},c_{1})
and the RHS by
Q(x,y,z)
.
The ideal
\mathcal{J}
(respectively of
\mathcal{I}
) generated by the coefficients of
G_{2}
(respectively of
F_{2}
) is exactly the idea generated by the determinants of all 2-dimensional minors of the symmetric matrix of
L
(respectively of
Q
).
Since
L((x,y,z)\cdot\Theta(s))=Q(x,y,z)
, we have
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathcal{J}
.
By symmetry, we conclude that
\mathcal{I}=\mathcal{J}
.
This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Now we are ready to prove Proposition
4.1

Proof.

Let
\tau\colon X^{\prime}\rightarrow X
be the projective bimeromorphic morphism induced by the functorial desingularization of
S
, as shown at the beginning of Subsection
3.B
.
Then, by replacing
X
with
X^{\prime}
,
S
with its strict transform
S^{\prime}
in
X^{\prime}
, and
C
with the union of its preimage in
S^{\prime}
and the intersection of
S^{\prime}
with the
\tau
-exceptional locus,
we may assume that
S
is smooth.
By applying Corollary
4.6
and then Lemma
4.7
, we may assume that
X
has double-point singularities along
S
.

It particular, locally around a point
o_{X}\in S
,
X
is the hypersurface in
\mathbb{D}^{3}\times S
, defined by the equation

F(x,y,z,s)=x^{2}+F_{2}(y,z,s)+F_{3}(y,z,s)+R(y,z,s)=0,

where
(x,y,z)
are the coordinates of
\mathbb{D}^{3}
and
s\in S
.
Moreover, for
i=2,3
,
F_{i}(y,z,s)
is a homogeneous polynomial of degree
i
in
(y,z)
, with coefficients as holomorphic functions on
S
.
The function
R(y,z,s)
is holomorphic and its terms have degrees at least 4 in
(y,z)
.

Since
X
has canonical singularities around general points of
S
, by Lemma
2.2

we see that either
F_{2}
or
F_{3}
is not identically zero.
Hence we can define the ideal sheaf
\mathcal{I}
as in Lemma
4.9
.

We only treat the case when
F_{2}\neq 0
.
The other case can be proved by the same method.
Let
p\colon X^{\prime}\rightarrow X
be the projective bimeromorphic morphism induced by the blowup of
S
at
\mathcal{I}
.
Let
S^{\prime}
be the strict transform of
S
in
X^{\prime}
, and let
E
be the divisor in
S^{\prime}
defined by
(p|_{S^{\prime}})^{*}\mathcal{I}
.
Then, as shown in Subsection
3.B
, for any point
o^{\prime}
in
S^{\prime}
,
there is a neighborhood
U^{\prime}\subseteq X^{\prime}
of
o^{\prime}
,
such that
U^{\prime}
is the hypersurface in
\mathbb{D}^{3}\times(S^{\prime}\cap U^{\prime})
, defined by the function

G(x^{\prime},y^{\prime},z^{\prime},s^{\prime})=x^{\prime 2}+a(s^{\prime})\cdot G_{2}(y^{\prime},z^{\prime},s^{\prime})+W(y^{\prime},z^{\prime},s^{\prime}),

where
a(s^{\prime})
is a generator of
\mathcal{O}_{S^{\prime}}(-E)
on
S^{\prime}\cap U^{\prime}
,
G_{2}(y^{\prime},z^{\prime},s^{\prime})
is a nonzero polynomial in
(y^{\prime},z^{\prime})
at any points
s^{\prime}\in S^{\prime}\cap U^{\prime}
,
and
W(y^{\prime},z^{\prime},s^{\prime})=0\operatorname{mod}\,(y^{\prime},z^{\prime})^{3}
.

We denote by
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
the ideal sheaf of the closed subset
E\cup(p|_{S^{\prime}})^{-1}(C)
in
S^{\prime}
.
Let
Y\rightarrow X^{\prime}
be the projective bimeromorphic morphism induced by the functorial principalization of
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
on
S^{\prime}
,
and let
f\colon Y\rightarrow X
be the natural morphism.
We denote by
S_{Y}
the strict transform of
S^{\prime}
in
Y
,
and by
H_{Y}\subseteq S_{Y}
the preimage of
E\cup(p|_{S^{\prime}})^{-1}(C)
.
Then
H_{Y}
can be seen as a reduced divisor in
S_{Y}
and
(S_{Y},H_{Y})
is a snc pair.
Furthermore, for any point
o\in S_{Y}
, there is a neighborhood
U\subseteq Y
of
o
, such that
U
is a hypersurface in
\mathbb{D}^{3}\times(S_{Y}\cap U)
defined by an equation of standard form with respect to
(S_{Y}\cap U,H_{Y}\cap U)
.
In addition,
Y
has the same type of canonical singularities at every point of
S_{Y}\setminus H_{Y}
,
and
H_{Y}
contains the intersection of
S_{Y}
with the
f
-exceptional locus.
This completes the proof of the proposition.
∎

5.
Further improvement of defining equations

Let
X
be a complex analytic variety and let
S\subseteq X_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sing}}}
be an irreducible component of codimension 2 in
X
.
In the previous section, we reduce the local situation to the case when
X
is isomorphic to a hypersurface of
\mathbb{D}^{3}\times S
, and the hypersurface is defined by an equation of standard form.
In this section, we will perform some bimeromorphic transforms on
X
,
so that we can improve further the shape of the equation.
After we prove some elementary results in Subsection
5.A
, we will work under the following setup.

Setup 5.1
.

Let
X
be a complex analytic variety of dimension
M+2
,
and let
S\subseteq X_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sing}}}
be an irreducible component of dimension
M
.
Let
o\in S
be a point and we assume that
S
is an open neighborhood of the origin
o=\mathbf{0}_{M}
in
\mathbb{C}^{M}
.
We denote by
(T_{1},...,T_{M})
the coordinates of
S
and we define
H_{i}=\{T_{i}=0\}
.
Let
H=H_{1}+\cdots+H_{n}
, where
n\geq 0
is an integer.
Assume that
X
is a neighborhood of
\{\mathbf{0}_{3}\}\times S
, in the hypersurface in
\mathbb{D}^{3}\times S
, defined by an equation of standard form with respect to
(S,H)
, see the beginning Section
4
.
Furthermore, the composite inclusion
S\subseteq X\subseteq\mathbb{D}^{3}\times S
is identified with
\{\mathbf{0}_{3}\}\times S\subseteq\mathbb{D}^{3}\times S
.
Assume that
X
has the same type of canonical singularities at points of
S\setminus H
.
We will shrink
X
and
S
freely around
o\in S
.
In particular, for every unit holomorphic function on
\mathbb{D}^{3}\times S
, we may assume that it admits logarithms.
In addition, for a holomorphic function
p(x,y,z,s)
on
\mathbb{D}^{3}\times S
of the shape

p(x,y,z,s)=p_{0}+q(x,y,z,s),

where
p_{0}\neq 0
is a complex number and
q(0,0,0,s)=0
,
we can assume that
p
is a unit function.
Throughout this section,
we will make these assumptions without the specification of shrinking
S
.

The following proposition is the main objective of in this section.

Proposition 5.2
.

With the notation of Setup
5.1
, up to shrinking
S
, there is an integer
N>0
such that the following properties hold.

(1)

Assume that
X
has
A
-type or
E
-type singularities at points of
S\setminus H
.
Let
f\colon Y\rightarrow X
be the composition of a sequence of blowups, such that each center is a component
H_{i}
of
H
, see Subsection
3.C
.
In particular, we can identify
S
with its strict transform in
Y
.
Assume that for each
i=1,...,n
, there are at least
N
blowups in the sequence whose centers are
H_{i}
.
Then there is a neighborhood
U\subseteq Y
of
S\subseteq Y
, such that
U
is isomorphic to a neighborhood of
\{\mathbf{0}_{3}\}\times S
in the hypersurface in
\mathbb{D}^{3}\times S
defined by a function of one of the following shapes

\displaystyle F(x,y,z,s)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x^{2}+\alpha(s)y^{2}+\beta(s)z^{l},\ l\geq 2,

\displaystyle F(x,y,z,s)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x^{2}+\alpha(s)y^{3}+\beta(s)\cdot y(\gamma(s)z+u(y,s)y)^{3},

\displaystyle F(x,y,z,s)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x^{2}+\alpha(s)y^{3}+\beta(s)\cdot(\gamma(s)z+u(y,s)y)^{l},\ l=4,5.

(2)

Assume that
X
has
D
-type singularities at points of
S\setminus H
.
Let
f_{1}\colon X_{1}\rightarrow X
be the composition of blowups at the
H_{i}
’s as in Subsection
3.C
,
such that for each
i=1,...,n
, there is exactly one blowup whose center is
H_{i}
.
Let
f_{2}\colon X_{2}\rightarrow X_{1}
be the basechange over
S[\sqrt[6]{T_{1}},...,\sqrt[6]{T_{n}}]
.
We define
\overline{S}=f_{2}^{-1}((f_{1})^{-1}_{*}S)
and define
\overline{H}_{i}
as the preimage of
H_{i}
in
\overline{S}
.
Let
f_{3}\colon Y\rightarrow X_{2}
be the composition of a sequence of blowups at the
\overline{H}_{i}
’s as in Subsection
3.C
.
Assume that for each
i=1,...,n
, there are at least
N
blowups in the sequence whose centers are
\overline{H}_{i}
.
Let
f\colon Y\rightarrow X
be the natural morphism,
let
\overline{S}_{Y}
be the strict transform of
\overline{S}
in
Y
.
Then there is a neighborhood
U\subseteq Y
of
\overline{S}_{Y}
,
such that
U
is isomorphic to a neighborhood of
\{\mathbf{0}_{3}\}\times\overline{S}_{Y}
in the hypersurface in
\mathbb{D}^{3}\times\overline{S}_{Y}
defined by a function of the shape

\displaystyle F(x,y,z,s)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x^{2}+\alpha(s)y(\gamma(s)z+u(y,s)y)^{2}+\beta(s)y^{l},\ l\geq 3.

Here
(x,y,z)
is the coordinates of
\mathbb{D}^{3}
,
l
is an integer,
\alpha(s)
,
\beta(s)
and
\gamma(s)
are holomorphic functions on
S
(or on
\overline{S}
) whose zero loci are contained in
H
(or in
\sum\overline{H}_{i}
), and
u(y,s)
is a holomorphic function.

In addition, regarded as a holomorphic function on
U
, the Cartier divisor defined by
T_{i}=0
is equal to the
f
-exceptional divisor defined by the ideal sheaf
f^{-1}\mathcal{I}(H_{i})\cdot\mathcal{O}_{Y}
,
where
\mathcal{I}(H_{i})
is the ideal sheaf of
H_{i}
in
X
.

We note that
U
in the previous proposition is not assumed to be the whole hypersurface in
\mathbb{D}^{3}\times S
(or in
\mathbb{D}^{3}\times\overline{S}_{Y}
) for the following reason.
In application, we shrink
S
to get an open subset
S^{\prime}\subseteq S
so that the proposition holds on
S^{\prime}
.
In particular, for example for the case (1),
we can get an open neighborhood
W
of the strict transform
S^{\prime\prime}
of
S^{\prime}
in
Y
so that
W
is isomorphic to a hypersurface in
\mathbb{D}^{3}\times S^{\prime\prime}
.
If we need to shrink
X
to get an open neighborhood
X^{\prime}
of
S^{\prime}
in
X
,
then we need to set
U=f^{-1}(X^{\prime})\cap W
in order that
f(U)
is contained in
X^{\prime}
.

We will prove the proposition in the remainder of the section, by discussing according to the singularity type of
X
at points of
S\setminus H
.

Proof of Proposition
5.2
.

The last paragraph in the statement follows from Remark
3.5
.
We can deduce the remainder part of proposition by combining the results from Lemma
5.5
to Lemma
5.9
.
∎

5.A.
Factorization lemmas

We prove two statements on holomorphic functions in several variables, which enable us to factorize certain functions.

Lemma 5.3
.

Let
F(y,z,s)=y(y^{2}+a(y,z,s)yz+b(y,z,s)z^{2})+b(y,z,s)^{2}R(y,z,s)
be a holomorphic function defined in a neighborhood of
\mathbf{0}_{2+N}\in\mathbb{C}^{2+N}
, where
N\geq 0
is an integer,
(y,z)
is the first two coordinates of
\mathbb{C}^{2+N}
, and
s\in\mathbb{C}^{N}
is a point which represents the last
N
coordinates of
\mathbb{C}^{2+N}
.
Assume that the terms of
R
have degrees at least
4
in
(y,z)
.
Then, in a neighborhood of
\mathbf{0}_{2+N}
,
there is a factorization
F=G_{1}G_{2}
with
G_{1}=y+b(y,z,s)c(y,z,s)

and

G_{2}=y^{2}+a(y,z,s)yz+b(y,z,s)z^{2}+b(y,z,s)\cdot d(y,z,s),

such that
c(y,z,s)
is divisible by
z^{2}

and that
d(y,z,s)=0\operatorname{mod}\,(y,z)^{3}
.

Proof.

We can write

R(y,z,s)=y^{3}R_{0}(y,z,s)+y^{2}z^{2}\cdot R_{1}(z,s)+yz^{3}\cdot R_{2}(z,s)+z^{4}\cdot R_{3}(y,z,s),

where
R_{0}(y,z,s)=0\operatorname{mod}\,(y,z)
.
Then we have

\displaystyle F(y,z,s)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle y\Big(\big(1+b(y,z,s)^{2}R_{0}(y,z,s)\big)y^{2}

\displaystyle+\big(a(y,z,s)+z\cdot b(y,z,s)^{2}\cdot R_{1}(z,s)\big)yz

\displaystyle+b(y,z,s)\big(1+z\cdot b(y,z,s)\cdot R_{2}(z,s)\big)z^{2}\Big)

\displaystyle+b(y,z,s)^{2}z^{4}\cdot R_{3}(y,z,s).

We note that
1+b(y,z,s)^{2}R_{0}(y,z,s)=1\operatorname{mod}\,(y,z)
.
In particular, it is a unit in a neighborhood of
\mathbf{0}_{N+2}
.
Hence, up to replacing

(1+b(y,z,s)^{2}R_{0}(y,z,s))^{-1}F(y,z,s)
by
F(y,z,s)
,

\big(1+b(y,z,s)^{2}R_{0}(y,z,s)\big)^{-1}\cdot\big(a(y,z,s)+z\cdot b(y,z,s)^{2}\cdot R_{1}(z,s)\big)\mbox{ by }a(y,z,s),

\big(1+b(y,z,s)^{2}R_{0}(y,z,s)\big)^{-1}\cdot\big(1+z\cdot b(y,z,s)\cdot R_{2}(z,s)\big)\cdot b(y,z,s)\mbox{ by }b(y,z,s),

we may assume that
R(y,z,s)
is divisible by
z^{4}
.
In particular, we can write
R(y,z,s)=z^{4}R_{3}(y,z,s).

We define the following holomorphic functions in
(y,z,s)
,

h(y,z,s)=y^{2}+a(y,z,s)yz+b(y,z,s)z^{2}\mbox{ and }p(y,z,s)=-y-a(y,z,s)z.

Then we have

p(y,z,s)y+h(y,z,s)=b(y,z,s)z^{2}.

Up to shrinking the domain of
F
, we may assume that

1+4p(y,z,s)R_{3}(y,z,s)
admits a square root
\delta(y,z,s)
of the shape

\delta(y,z,s)=1+\sum_{i=1}^{+\infty}\binom{\frac{1}{2}}{i}\cdot\Big(4p(y,z,s)R_{3}(y,z,s)\Big)^{i}.

Then
-1+\delta(y,z,s)
is divisible by
p(y,z,s)
.
We set

\gamma(y,z,s)=\frac{1}{2}\cdot p(y,z,s)^{-1}\cdot(-1+\delta(y,z,s)).

Then
\gamma
is a holomorphic function and satisfies the equation

p\gamma^{2}+\gamma-R_{3}=0.

Let
c(y,z,s)=z^{2}\cdot\gamma(y,z,s)
and
d(y,z,s)=z^{2}\cdot\gamma(y,z,s)\cdot p(y,z,s)
.
Then we have

\displaystyle(y+bc)(h+bd)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle yh+b(yd+hc)+b^{2}dc

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle yh+bz^{2}\gamma\cdot(py+h)+b^{2}z^{4}\gamma^{2}p

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle yh+b^{2}z^{4}\gamma+b^{2}z^{4}\gamma^{2}p

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle yh+b^{2}z^{4}R_{3}+b^{2}z^{4}\cdot(-R_{3}+\gamma+p\gamma^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle F

This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Corollary 5.4
.

Let
F(y,z,s)=y(y^{2}+z^{3})+z^{5}R(y,z,s)
be a holomorphic function defined in a neighborhood of
\mathbf{0}_{2+N}\in\mathbb{C}^{2+N}
, where
N\geq 0
is an integer,
(y,z)
is the first two coordinates of
\mathbb{C}^{2+N}
,
and
s\in\mathbb{C}^{N}
is a point which represents the last
N
coordinates of
\mathbb{C}^{2+N}
.
Then, in a neighborhood of
\mathbf{0}_{2+N}
,
there is a factorization
F=G_{1}G_{2}
with
G_{1}=y+c(y,z,s)
and
G_{2}=y^{2}+z^{3}+d(y,z,s)+e(s)yz^{2}
,
such that
c(y,z,s)
is divisible by
z^{2}
, and that terms of
d(y,z,s)
have degrees at least 4 in
(y,z)
.

Proof.

We can write
R(y,z,s)=C(s)+R_{1}(y,z,s)
, where the terms of
R_{1}(y,z,s)
have degrees at least
1
in
(y,z)
.
It follows that

\displaystyle H(y,z,s)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle(y+C(s)\cdot z^{2})(y^{2}+z^{3}-C(s)\cdot z^{2}y+C(s)^{2}\cdot z^{4})

\displaystyle\ =

\displaystyle y(y^{2}+z^{3})+C(s)\cdot z^{5}+C(s)^{3}\cdot z^{6}

\displaystyle\ =

\displaystyle F(y,z,s)+z^{5}\cdot(C(s)^{3}z-R_{1}(y,z,s))

We set
{y^{\prime}}=y+C(s)z^{2}
.
Then we have

\displaystyle H(y,z,s)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{y^{\prime}}\Big(({y^{\prime}}-Cz^{2})^{2}+z^{3}-Cz^{2}({y^{\prime}}-Cz^{2})+C^{2}z^{4}\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{y^{\prime}}\Big({y^{\prime}}^{2}-3C{y^{\prime}}z^{2}+z^{3}(1+3C^{2}z)\Big).

Thus we can write

F(y,z,s)={y^{\prime}}\left({y^{\prime}}^{2}-3C{y^{\prime}}z^{2}+z^{3}(1+3C^{2}z)\right)+z^{5}R_{2}({y^{\prime}},z,s)

for some holomorphic function
R_{2}
, whose terms have degrees at least 1 in
(y^{\prime},z)
.
Let
u=z
, then we have

F(y,z,s)=\overline{F}({y^{\prime}},z,s,u)={y^{\prime}}({y^{\prime}}^{2}+a(u,s)\cdot{y^{\prime}}z+n(u,s)u\cdot z^{2})+u^{2}\cdot z^{3}R_{2}({y^{\prime}},z,s),

where
a(u,s)=-3C(s)u
, and
n(u,s)=(1+3C(s)^{2}u)
is a unit.
Hence we can apply Lemma
5.3
to
\overline{F}(y^{\prime},z,s,u)
,
and deduce a factorization
\overline{F}=\overline{G}_{1}\overline{G}_{2}
with
\overline{G}_{1}={y^{\prime}}+\overline{c}({y^{\prime}},z,s)
such that
\overline{c}
is divisible by
z^{2}
.
Furthermore, we have

\overline{G}_{2}={y^{\prime}}^{2}-3C(s){y^{\prime}}z^{2}+z^{3}(1+3C(s)^{2}z)+\overline{d}({y^{\prime}},z,s),

where the terms of
\overline{d}
have degrees at least
4
in
(y^{\prime},z)
.
Since
y^{\prime}=y+C(s)z^{2}
, we obtain a factorization
{F}={G}_{1}{G}_{2}
with
G_{1}={y}+c(y,z,s)
and
G_{2}=y^{2}+z^{3}+d(y,z,s)+e(s)yz^{2}
,
such that
c
is divisible by
z^{2}
and that the terms of
d
have degree at least
4
in
(y,z)
.
This completes the proof of the corollary.
∎

5.B.
Case of
A
-type singularities

In this subsection, we treat Proposition
5.2
in the case when
X
has
A
-type singularities at points of
S\setminus H
.

Lemma 5.5
.

Proposition
5.2
holds if
X
has
A
-type singularities at points of
S\setminus H
.

Proof.

We may assume that
X\subseteq\mathbb{D}^{3}\times S
is defined by an equation of standard form with respect to
(S,H)
, which is of the shape

x^{2}+F_{2}(y,z,s)+F_{3}(y,z,s)+R(y,z,s)=0,

where
F_{2}
and
F_{3}
are homogeneous in
(y,z)
of degree 2 and 3 respectively, and
R
is a holomorphic function such that
R(y,z,s)=0\operatorname{mod}\,(y,z)^{3}
.
By assumption, when
s\not\in H
, the term
F_{2}(y,z,s)
is not zero (see Lemma
2.2
).
Since the defining equation is of standard form with respect to
(S,H)
,
up to a linear change of the coordinates
(y,z)
, we may assume that

F_{2}(y,z,s)=a(s)(y^{2}+b(s)yz+c(s)z^{2})
, where the zero locus of
a
is contained in
H
.
By blowing up
X
at the
H_{i}
’s for several times,
we may assume that
a(s)
divides
F_{3}
and
R
(see Remark
3.7
).
Hence
X
is defined by

x^{2}+a(s)\Big(y^{2}+b(s)yz+c(s)z^{2}+R^{\prime}(y,z,s)\Big)=0,

where
R^{\prime}(y,z.s)=0\operatorname{mod}\,(y,z)^{3}
.
By applying Weierstrass preparation theorem to the function in the parenthesis after
a(s)
above,
with respect to the variable
y
, we see that
X
is defined by

x^{2}+a(s)\cdot(\mathrm{{unit}})\cdot(y^{2}+y\cdot R_{1}(z,s)+R_{2}(z,s))=0,

for some holomorphic functions
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
.
Replacing
y
by
y-\frac{1}{2}R_{1}(z,s)
, the defining equation becomes

x^{2}+a(s)\cdot(\mathrm{{unit}})\cdot\left(y^{2}+z^{e}\cdot R_{3}(z,s)\right)=0,

where
e\geq 2
is an integer and
R_{3}
is not divisible by
z
.

By assumption, there is some integer
l\geq 1
such that
X
has
A_{l}
-type singularities at any point
s\in S\setminus H
.
Hence
e=l+1
, and
if
b(s)
is the term in
R_{3}
of degree
0
in
z
, then the zero locus of
b(s)
is contained in
H
.
Thus, by blowing up
X
at the
H_{i}
’s,
we may assume that
b(s)
divides
R_{3}(z,s)
, see Remark
3.7
.
Then the defining equation is of the shape

x^{2}+a(s)\cdot(\mathrm{unit})\cdot y^{2}+b(s)\cdot(\mathrm{unit})\cdot z^{l+1}=0,

where the zero locus of
b(s)
is contained in
H
.
Hence, up to a change of coordinates, the defining equation becomes

x^{2}+\alpha(s)y^{2}+\beta(s)z^{l+1}=0,

as in the statement of Proposition
5.2
.
In the end, we note that if we continue blowing up
X
at some
H_{i}
, then the defining equation remains the same shape.
This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

5.C.
Case of
E
-type singularities

In this subsection, we treat Proposition
5.2
in the case when
X
has
E
-type singularities at points of
S\setminus H
.
We will discuss the cases of
E_{6}
,
E_{7}
and
E_{8}
separately in three lemmas.

Lemma 5.6
.

Proposition
5.2
holds if
X
has
E_{6}
-type singularities at points of
S\setminus H
.

Proof.

We may assume that
X\subseteq\mathbb{D}^{3}\times S
is defined by an equation of standard form with respect to
(S,H)
, which is of the shape

x^{2}+F_{2}(y,z,s)+F_{3}(y,z,s)+R(y,z,s)=0,

where
F_{2}
and
F_{3}
are homogeneous in
(y,z)
of degree 2 and 3 respectively, and
R(y,z,s)=0\operatorname{mod}\,(y,z)^{4}
.
By Lemma
2.2
, when
s\not\in H
, the term
F_{2}
is zero and
F_{3}
is a cube.
It follows that
F_{2}=0
.
Moreover, since the equation is of standard form with respect to
(S,H)
,
up to shrinking
X
and up to a linear change of the coordinates
(y,z)
,
we can assume that

F_{3}
is of the shape
F_{3}(y,z,s)=a(s)(y-u(s)z)^{3}
for some holomorphic function
a(s)
whose zero locus is contained in
H
,
and for some holomorphic function
u(s)
.
Replacing
y
by
y+u(s)z
, we may assume that

F_{3}(y,z,s)=a(s)y^{3}.

By blowing up
X
for several times at the
H_{i}
’s as in Subsection
3.C
, we may assume that
a(s)
divides
R(y,z,s)
, see Remark
3.7
.
Then we can write

\displaystyle F_{3}(y,z,s)+R(y,z,s)=a(s)\cdot(y^{3}+\cdots),

By applying Weierstrass preparation theorem to the series in the parenthesis after
a(s)
in the equation above, with respect to
y
,
we can write

\displaystyle F_{3}(y,z,s)+R(y,z,s)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\mathrm{unit})\cdot a(s)\cdot\Big(y^{3}+\omega_{0}(z,s)z^{2}y^{2}

\displaystyle+\omega_{1}(z,s)z^{3+p}y+\omega_{2}(z,s)z^{4+q}\Big),

where
q,p\geq 0
are integers,
\omega_{1}
and
\omega_{2}
are either zero or non divisible by
z
.
Up to replacing
y
by
y-\frac{1}{3}\omega_{0}(z,s)z^{2}
, we can assume that

F_{3}(y,z,s)+R(y,z,s)=(\mathrm{unit})\cdot a(s)\cdot\Big(y^{3}+\omega_{1}(z,s)z^{3+p}y+\omega_{2}(z,s)z^{4+q}\Big).

By merging the terms of
\omega_{1}(z,s)z^{3+p}y
divisible by
z^{4+q}
into
\omega_{2}(z,s)z^{4+q}
, we can assume that
q\geq p
and write

F_{3}(y,z,s)+R(y,z,s)=(\mathrm{unit})\cdot a(s)\cdot\Big(y^{3}+v(z,s)z^{3+p}y+w(y,z,s)z^{4+q}\Big),

such that either
v
is zero or
z
does not divide
v
.
Moreover, the terms of
v(z,s)
have degrees at most
q-p
in
z
.
In addition, either
w(y,z,s)
is zero, or
w(0,0,s)\neq 0
for a general point
s\in S
.

Since
X
has
E_{6}
-type singularities at points of
S\setminus H
, by Lemma
2.2
,
we deduce that
q=p=0
, and
v(z,s)=v(s)
is a holomorphic function on
S
.
Lemma
2.2
also implies that
w(y,z,s)=b(s)+b^{\prime}(y,z,s)
, where
the zero locus of
b(s)
is contained in
H
and
b^{\prime}(y,z,s)=0\operatorname{mod}\,(y,z)
.
By blowing up
X
at the
H_{i}
’s for several times as in Subsection
3.C
,
we may assume that
w(y,z,s)=\eta(y,z,s)\cdot b(s)
, where
\eta
is a unit, see Remark
3.7
.

Let
T=T_{1}\cdots T_{n}
.
Since the zero locus of
b(s)
is contained in
H=\{T=0\}
, there is an integer
e^{\prime}\geq 0
such that
T^{e^{\prime}}
is divisible by
b(s)
.
Let
e=3e^{\prime}
.
By blowing up
X
at each
H_{i}
for
2e
times as in Subsection
3.C
,
the defining function
F(x,y,z,s)
becomes

F(x,y,z,s)=x^{2}+(\mathrm{unit})\cdot a(s)\cdot\Big(T^{2e}y^{3}+T^{4e}v(s)z^{3}y+\eta(y,z,s)\cdot b(s)\cdot T^{4e}z^{4}\Big),

where, by abuse of notation, we still denote
\eta(T^{2e}y,T^{2e}z,s)
by
\eta(y,z,s)
.
Since
e-2e^{\prime}=e^{\prime}
, by the choice of
e^{\prime}
, there is a function
m(s)
such that

b(s)\cdot m(s)\cdot T^{2e^{\prime}}=v(s)\cdot T^{e}.

Hence we can write

\displaystyle F(x,y,z,s)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x^{2}+(\mathrm{unit})\cdot a(s)\cdot\Big(T^{2e}y^{3}

\displaystyle+b(s)\cdot m(s)\cdot(T^{e}z)^{3}\cdot T^{2e^{\prime}}y

\displaystyle+\eta(y,z,s)\cdot b(s)\cdot(T^{e}z)^{4}\Big).

We set

\zeta=T^{e}z+\frac{1}{4}\eta(y,z,s)^{-1}m(s)\cdot T^{2e^{\prime}}y,

so that

\displaystyle F(x,y,z,s)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x^{2}+(\mathrm{unit})\cdot a(s)\cdot\Big(T^{2e}y^{3}+\nu_{1}(y,z,s)\cdot b(s)(T^{2e^{\prime}}y)^{4}

\displaystyle+\nu_{2}(y,z,s)\cdot b(s)\zeta\cdot(T^{2e^{\prime}}y)^{3}

\displaystyle+\nu_{3}(y,z,s)\cdot b(s)\zeta^{2}\cdot(T^{2e^{\prime}}y)^{2}+\eta(y,z,s)\cdot b(s)\zeta^{4}\Big),

for some holomorphic functions
\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\nu_{3}
.
We remark that
T^{2e}y^{3}=(T^{2e^{\prime}}y)^{3}
and we consider the following part in the last expression on
F(x,y,z,s)
above,

\displaystyle(T^{2e^{\prime}}y)^{3}+\nu_{1}(y,z,s)\cdot b(s)(T^{2e^{\prime}}y)^{4}

\displaystyle+\nu_{2}(y,z,s)\cdot b(s)\zeta\cdot(T^{2e^{\prime}}y)^{3}

\displaystyle+\nu_{3}(y,z,s)\cdot b(s)\zeta^{2}\cdot(T^{2e^{\prime}}y)^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\mathrm{unit})\cdot(T^{2e^{\prime}}y)^{3}+\nu_{3}(y,z,s)\cdot b(s)\zeta^{2}\cdot(T^{2e^{\prime}}y)^{2}.

It follows that there are some holomorphic functions
\mu(y,z,s)
and
\sigma(y,z,s)
such that the function
F
can be written as

\displaystyle F(x,y,z,s)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x^{2}+(\mathrm{unit})\cdot a(s)\cdot\Big((\mathrm{unit})\cdot(T^{2e^{\prime}}y+\mu(y,z,s)\zeta^{2})^{3}

\displaystyle+\sigma(y,z,s)\cdot b(s)\zeta^{4}+\eta(y,z,s)\cdot b(s)\zeta^{4}\Big).

Furthermore,
\sigma(y,z,s)=0\operatorname{mod}\,(y,z)
.
Hence the function
F
can be written as

F(x,y,z,s)=x^{2}+a(s)\cdot(\mathrm{unit})\cdot\Big((\mathrm{unit})\cdot(T^{2e^{\prime}}y+\mu(y,z,s)\zeta^{2})^{3}+(\mathrm{unit})\cdot b(s)\zeta^{4}\Big).

We recall that
\zeta=T^{e}z+\frac{1}{4}\eta(y,z,s)^{-1}m(s)\cdot T^{2e^{\prime}}y
.
Thus there is some holomorphic function
\mu^{\prime}(y,z,s)
such that

\displaystyle T^{2e^{\prime}}y+\mu(y,z,s)\zeta^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\mathrm{unit})\cdot T^{2e^{\prime}}y+\mu(y,z,s)T^{2e}z^{2}

\displaystyle(\mathrm{unit})\cdot T^{2e^{\prime}}\Big(y+\mu^{\prime}(y,z,s)T^{2e-2e^{\prime}}z^{2}\Big).

If we set
\overline{y}=y+\mu^{\prime}(y,z,s)T^{2e-2e^{\prime}}z^{2}
,
then
y-\overline{y}
is divisible by
T^{2e-2e^{\prime}}z^{2}
.
We deduce that

\displaystyle\zeta

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle T^{e}z+\frac{1}{4}\eta(y,z,s)^{-1}m(s)\cdot T^{2e^{\prime}}y

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle T^{e}z+\frac{1}{4}\eta(y,z,s)^{-1}m(s)\cdot T^{2e^{\prime}}\overline{y}+\frac{1}{4}\eta(y,z,s)^{-1}m(s)\cdot T^{2e^{\prime}}(y-\overline{y})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\mathrm{unit})\cdot T^{e}z+\frac{1}{4}\eta(y,z,s)^{-1}m(s)\cdot T^{2e^{\prime}}\overline{y}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\mathrm{unit})\cdot\Big((\mathrm{unit})\cdot T^{e}z+m(s)\cdot T^{2e^{\prime}}\overline{y}\Big).

Therefore, up to replacing
\overline{y}
by
y
, the function
F
can be written as

\displaystyle F(x,y,z,s)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x^{2}+R_{1}(y,z,s)\cdot a(s)\cdot T^{2e}y^{3}

\displaystyle+R_{2}(y,z,s)\cdot a(s)b(s)\cdot(R_{3}(y,z,s)T^{e}z+m(s)\cdot T^{2e^{\prime}}y)^{4},

where
R_{1},R_{2},R_{3}
are units.
We let
R_{4}=R_{2}R_{1}^{-1}
and
R_{5}=R_{1}R_{4}^{-3}=R_{2}R_{4}^{-4}
, so that

\displaystyle F(x,y,z,s)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x^{2}+R_{1}R_{4}^{-3}\cdot a(s)\cdot T^{2e}(R_{4}y)^{3}

\displaystyle+R_{2}R_{4}^{-4}\cdot a(s)b(s)\cdot(R_{3}R_{4}T^{e}z+m(s)\cdot T^{2e^{\prime}}R_{4}y)^{4}.

Up to replacing
y
by
R_{4}^{-1}y
, we deduce that
F
is of the shape

\displaystyle F(x,y,z,s)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x^{2}+R_{5}\cdot a(s)\cdot T^{2e}y^{3}

\displaystyle+R_{5}\cdot a(s)b(s)\cdot(R_{3}R_{4}T^{e}z+m(s)\cdot T^{2e^{\prime}}y)^{4}.

Then
X
is defined by

\displaystyle R_{5}^{-1}x^{2}+a(s)\cdot T^{2e}y^{3}+a(s)b(s)\cdot(R_{3}R_{4}T^{e}z+m(s)\cdot T^{2e^{\prime}}y)^{4}=0.

Hence there is a change of coordinates so that
X
can be defined by

x^{2}+\alpha(s)y^{3}+\beta(s)(\gamma(s)z+u(y,s)y)^{4}=0,

where the zero loci of
\alpha(s)
,
\beta(s)
and
\gamma(s)
are contained in
H
.
In the end, we note that if we continue blowing up
X
at some
H_{i}
, then the defining equation remains the same shape.
This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

In the following lemma, we treat the case of
E_{8}
-type singularity.
Its proof proceeds in the same way as the one for the case of
E_{6}
-type singularity.

Lemma 5.7
.

Proposition
5.2
holds if
X
has
E_{8}
-type singularities at points of
S\setminus H
.

Proof.

As in the case of Lemma
5.6
, we can reduce to the situation when

X
is a hypersurface in
\mathbb{D}^{3}\times S
defined by the equation

F(x,y,z,s)=x^{2}+F_{3}(y,z,s)+R(y,z,s)=0,

with

F_{3}(y,z,s)+R(y,z,s)=(\mathrm{unit})\cdot a(s)\cdot\Big(y^{3}+v(z,s)z^{3+p}y+w(y,z,s)z^{4+q}\Big),

where
q\geq p\geq 0
are integers.
Either
v
is zero or
z
does not divide
v
. Furthermore,
the terms of
v(z,s)
have degrees at most
q-p
in
z
.
In addition, either
w(y,z,s)
is zero, or
w(0,0,s)\neq 0
for a general point
s\in S
.

Since
X
has
E_{8}
singularities at points of
S\setminus H
, by Lemma
2.2
,
we deduce that
q=p=1
, and
v(z,s)=v(s)
is a holomorphic function on
S
.
Lemma
2.2
also implies that
w(y,z,s)=b(s)+b^{\prime}(y,z,s)
such that
the zero locus of
b(s)
is contained in
H
and that
b^{\prime}(y,z,s)=0\operatorname{mod}\,(y,z)
.
By blowing up
X
at the
H_{i}
’s for several times as in Subsection
3.C
,
we may assume that
w(y,z,s)=\eta(y,z,s)\cdot b(s)
, where
\eta
is a unit, see Remark
3.7
.

Let
T=T_{1}\cdots T_{n}
.
Since the zero locus of the function
b(s)
is contained in
H=\{T=0\}
, there is an integer
e^{\prime}\geq 0
such that
T^{e^{\prime}}
is divisible by
b(s)
.
We define

e=5d=15e^{\prime}.

By blowing up
X
at each
H_{i}
for
e
times, as in Subsection
3.C
,
the function
F
becomes

\displaystyle F(x,y,z,s)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x^{2}+(\mathrm{unit})\cdot a(s)\cdot\Big(T^{e}y^{3}

\displaystyle+T^{3e}v(s)z^{4}y+\eta(y,z,s)\cdot b(s)\cdot T^{3e}z^{5}\Big),

where, by abuse of notation, we still denote
\eta(T^{e}y,T^{e}z,s)
by
\eta(y,z,s)
.
Since
e=5d
, we remark that, in the last line of the previous equation,

T^{3e}v(s)z^{4}y+\eta(y,z,s)\cdot b(s)\cdot T^{3e}z^{5}=(T^{3d}z)^{4}\cdot v(s)\cdot T^{3d}y+\eta(y,z,s)\cdot b(s)\cdot(T^{3d}z)^{5}.

Since
3d-5e^{\prime}=4e^{\prime}
, by the choice of
e^{\prime}
, there is a function
m(s)
such that

b(s)\cdot m(s)\cdot T^{5e^{\prime}}=v(s)\cdot T^{3d}.

Hence we can write

\displaystyle F(x,y,z,s)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x^{2}+(\mathrm{unit})\cdot a(s)\cdot\Big(T^{e}y^{3}

\displaystyle+b(s)\cdot m(s)\cdot(T^{3d}z)^{4}\cdot T^{5e^{\prime}}y

\displaystyle+\eta(y,z,s)\cdot b(s)\cdot(T^{3d}z)^{5}\Big).

We set

\zeta=T^{3d}z+\frac{1}{5}\eta(y,z,s)^{-1}m(s)\cdot T^{5e^{\prime}}y,

so that

\displaystyle F(x,y,z,s)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x^{2}+(\mathrm{unit})\cdot a(s)\cdot\Big(T^{e}y^{3}+\nu_{1}(y,z,s)\cdot b(s)\cdot(T^{5e^{\prime}}y)^{5}

\displaystyle+\nu_{2}(y,z,s)\cdot b(s)\zeta\cdot(T^{5e^{\prime}}y)^{4}+\nu_{3}(y,z,s)\cdot b(s)\zeta^{2}\cdot(T^{5e^{\prime}}y)^{3}

\displaystyle+\nu_{4}(y,z,s)\cdot b(s)\zeta^{3}\cdot(T^{5e^{\prime}}y)^{2}+\eta(y,z,s)\cdot b(s)\zeta^{5}\Big),

for some holomorphic functions
\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\nu_{3},\nu_{4}
.
We remark that
T^{e}y^{3}=(T^{5e^{\prime}}y)^{3}
and we consider the following part in the last expression of
F(x,y,z,s)
above,

\displaystyle T^{e}y^{3}+\nu_{1}(y,z,s)\cdot b(s)(T^{5e^{\prime}}y)^{5}+\nu_{2}(y,z,s)\cdot b(s)\zeta\cdot(T^{5e^{\prime}}y)^{4}

\displaystyle+\nu_{3}(y,z,s)\cdot b(s)\zeta^{2}\cdot(T^{5e^{\prime}}y)^{3}+\nu_{4}(y,z,s)\cdot b(s)\zeta^{3}\cdot(T^{5e^{\prime}}y)^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\mathrm{unit})\cdot(T^{5e^{\prime}}y)^{3}+\nu_{4}(y,z,s)\cdot b(s)\zeta^{3}\cdot(T^{5e^{\prime}}y)^{2}.

Then there are some holomorphic functions
\mu(y,z,s)
and
\sigma(y,z,s)
such that the function
F
can be written as

\displaystyle F(x,y,z,s)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x^{2}+(\mathrm{unit})\cdot a(s)\cdot\Big((\mathrm{unit})\cdot(T^{5e^{\prime}}y+\mu(y,z,s)\zeta^{3})^{3}

\displaystyle+\sigma(y,z,s)\cdot b(s)\zeta^{6}+\eta(y,z,s)\cdot b(s)\zeta^{5}\Big).

Hence the function
F
can be written as

F(x,y,z,s)=x^{2}+a(s)\Big((\mathrm{unit})\cdot(T^{5e^{\prime}}y+\mu(y,z,s)\zeta^{3})^{3}+(\mathrm{unit})\cdot b(s)\zeta^{5}\Big).

We recall that
\zeta=T^{3d}z+\frac{1}{5}\eta(y,z,s)^{-1}m(s)\cdot T^{5e^{\prime}}y
.
Hence there is some holomorphic function
\mu^{\prime}(y,z,s)
such that

\displaystyle T^{5e^{\prime}}y+\mu(y,z,s)\zeta^{3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\mathrm{unit})\cdot T^{5e^{\prime}}y+\mu(y,z,s)\cdot T^{9d}z^{3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\mathrm{unit})\cdot T^{5e^{\prime}}\Big(y+\mu^{\prime}(y,z,s)\cdot T^{9d-5e^{\prime}}z^{3}\Big).

Let
\overline{y}=y+\mu^{\prime}(y,z,s)\cdot T^{9d-5e^{\prime}}z^{3}
.
Then
y-\overline{y}
is divisible by
T^{9d-5e^{\prime}}z^{3}
, and we have

\displaystyle\zeta

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle T^{3d}z+\frac{1}{5}\eta(y,z,s)^{-1}m(s)\cdot T^{5e^{\prime}}y

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle T^{3d}z+\frac{1}{5}\eta(y,z,s)^{-1}m(s)\cdot T^{5e^{\prime}}\overline{y}+\frac{1}{5}\eta(y,z,s)^{-1}m(s)\cdot T^{5e^{\prime}}(y-\overline{y})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\mathrm{unit})\cdot T^{3d}z+\frac{1}{5}\eta(y,z,s)^{-1}m(s)\cdot T^{5e^{\prime}}\overline{y}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\mathrm{unit})\cdot\Big((\mathrm{unit})\cdot T^{3d}z+m(s)\cdot T^{5e^{\prime}}\overline{y}\Big).

Up to replacing
\overline{y}
with
y
, we can write

\displaystyle F(x,y,z,s)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x^{2}+R_{1}(y,z,s)\cdot a(s)T^{e}y^{3}

\displaystyle+R_{2}(y,z,s)\cdot a(s)b(s)\cdot(R_{3}(y,z,s)T^{3d}z+m(s)\cdot T^{5e^{\prime}}y)^{5},

where
R_{1},R_{2},R_{3}
are units.
There is a holomorphic function
R_{4}
such that
R_{4}^{2}=R_{2}R_{1}^{-1}
.
Then we can set
R_{5}=R_{2}R_{4}^{-5}=R_{1}R_{4}^{-3}
, and we have

\displaystyle F(x,y,z,s)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x^{2}+R_{5}\cdot a(s)T^{e}(R_{4}y)^{3}

\displaystyle+R_{5}a(s)b(s)(R_{3}R_{4}T^{3d}z+m(s)T^{5e^{\prime}}R_{4}y)^{5}.

Up to replacing
y
by
R_{4}^{-1}y
, we see that
X
is defined by

R_{5}^{-1}x^{2}+a(s)T^{e}y^{3}+a(s)b(s)(R_{3}R_{4}T^{3d}z+m(s)T^{5e^{\prime}}y)^{5}=0.

Hence there is a change of coordinates so that the defining equation becomes

x^{2}+\alpha(s)y^{3}+\beta(s)(\gamma(s)z+u(y,s)y)^{5}=0,

where the zero locus of
\alpha(s)
,
\beta(s)
and
\gamma(s)
are contained in
H
.
In the end, we note that if we continue blowing up
X
at some
H_{i}
, then the defining equation remains the same shape.
This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

We will now treat the case of
E_{7}
-type singularity.
Its proof is slightly different from the ones of
E_{6}
-type or
E_{8}
-type singularities.

Lemma 5.8
.

Proposition
5.2
holds if
X
has
E_{7}
-type singularities at points of
S\setminus H
.

Proof.

As in the case of Lemma
5.6
, we can reduce to the situation when

X
is a hypersurface in
\mathbb{D}^{3}\times S
defined by the equation

F(x,y,z,s)=x^{2}+F_{3}(y,z,s)+R(y,z,s)=0,

with

F_{3}(y,z,s)+R(y,z,s)=(\mathrm{unit})\cdot a(s)\cdot\Big(y^{3}+v(z,s)z^{3+p}y+w(y,z,s)z^{4+q}\Big),

where
q\geq p\geq 0
are integers.
Either
v
is zero or
z
does not divide
v
. Furthermore,
the terms of
v(z,s)
have degrees at most
q-p
in
z
.
In addition, either
w(y,z,s)
is zero, or
w(0,0,s)\neq 0
for a general point
s\in S
.

Since
X
has
E_{7}
singularities at points of
S\setminus H
, by Lemma
2.2
,
we deduce that
p=0
and
q\geq 1
.
Lemma
2.2
also implies that
v(z,s)=b(s)+z\cdot\omega(z,s)
such that
the zero locus of
b(s)
is contained in
H
and that
\omega(z,s)
is a holomorphic function.
By blowing up
X
at the
H_{i}
’s for several times as in Subsection
3.C
,
we may assume that
v(z,s)=\eta(z,s)\cdot b(s)
, where
\eta
is a unit, see Remark
3.7
.
Up to multiplying
\eta
by a unit function,
we can assume that
b(s)=\prod_{i}T_{i}^{l_{i}}
for some integers
l_{1},...,l_{n}\geq 0
.
Then we have

F_{3}(y,z,s)+R(y,z,s)=(\mathrm{unit})\cdot a(s)\cdot\Big(y^{3}+\eta(z,s)b(s)yz^{3}+w(y,z,s)z^{4+q}\Big).

By blow up
X
at each
H_{i}
for
e_{i}
times as in Subsection
3.C
,
we can write

\displaystyle F(x,y,z,s)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x^{2}+(\mathrm{unit})\cdot a(s)\cdot\prod_{i}T_{i}^{e_{i}}\cdot\Big(y^{3}

\displaystyle+\prod_{i}T_{i}^{e_{i}+l_{i}}\eta(z,s)yz^{3}+\prod_{i}T_{i}^{qe_{i}+e_{i}}w(y,z,s)z^{4+q}\Big),

where by abuse of notation, we still write
\eta(z,s)
for
\eta(\prod_{i}T_{i}^{e_{i}}\cdot z,s)
and
w(y,z,s)
for
w(\prod_{i}T_{i}^{e_{i}}\cdot y,\prod_{i}T_{i}^{e_{i}}\cdot z,s)
.
We choose
e_{i}
so that
e_{i}+l_{i}=3e_{i}^{\prime}
for some integer
e_{i}^{\prime}>0
.
We note that

\displaystyle qe_{i}+e_{i}-(4+q)e_{i}^{\prime}=(q+1)e_{i}-\frac{1}{3}(4+q)(e_{i}+l_{i})=\frac{1}{3}(2q-1)e_{i}-\frac{1}{3}(4+q)l_{i}.

Since
q\geq 1
, the RHS above tends to
+\infty
if
e_{i}
tends to
+\infty
.
Hence we can pick
e_{i}
sufficiently large so that

\prod_{i}T_{i}^{(4+q)e_{i}^{\prime}}\mbox{ divides }\prod_{i}T_{i}^{qe_{i}+e_{i}}w(y,z,s).

Then there is some holomorphic function
m(y,z,s)
such that

\prod_{i}T_{i}^{qe_{i}+e_{i}}w(y,z,s)\cdot z^{4+q}=m(y,z,s)\cdot\prod_{i}T_{i}^{(4+q)e_{i}^{\prime}}\cdot z^{4+q}.

Let
\zeta=z\cdot\prod_{i}T_{i}^{e_{i}^{\prime}}
.
Then we have

\displaystyle F(x,y,z,s)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x^{2}+(\mathrm{unit})\cdot a(s)\cdot\prod_{i}T_{i}^{e_{i}}\cdot\Big(y^{3}+\eta(z,s)y\zeta^{3}+m(y,z,s)\zeta^{4+q}\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x^{2}+(\mathrm{unit})\cdot a(s)\cdot\prod_{i}T_{i}^{e_{i}}

\displaystyle\cdot\Big(y(\eta(z,s)^{-1}y^{2}+\zeta^{3})+\eta(z,s)^{-1}m(y,z,s)\zeta^{4+q}\Big).

Replacing
y
by
y\cdot\eta(z,s)^{\frac{1}{2}}
,
we can write

F(x,y,z,s)=x^{2}+(\mathrm{unit})\cdot a(s)\cdot\prod_{i}T_{i}^{e_{i}}\cdot\Big(y(y^{2}+\zeta^{3})+m^{\prime}(y,z,s)\zeta^{4+q}\Big)

for some holomorphic function
m^{\prime}(y,z,s)
.
By applying Corollary
5.4

to the function

y(y^{2}+\zeta^{3})+m^{\prime}(y,z,s)\zeta^{4+q},

it can be factorized into

\Big(y+c(y,\zeta,z,s)\Big)\Big(y^{2}+\zeta^{3}+\sigma(y,\zeta,z,s)+\nu(s)\cdot\zeta^{2}y\Big),

where
\zeta^{2}
divides
c(y,\zeta,z,s)
and
\sigma(y,\zeta,z,s)=0\operatorname{mod}\,(y,\zeta)^{4}
.
By replacing
y+c(y,\zeta,z,s)
with
y
,
the previous term becomes

\displaystyle y\Big(y^{2}+\nu_{1}(y,\zeta,z,s)y\zeta^{2}+(\mathrm{unit})\cdot\zeta^{3}+\nu_{2}(y,\zeta,z,s)\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle y\Big((\mathrm{unit})\cdot y^{2}+\nu_{3}(z,s)y\zeta^{2}+(\mathrm{unit})\cdot\zeta^{3}\Big),

where
\nu_{1},\nu_{2}
and
\nu_{3}
are holomorphic functions, and
\nu_{2}(y,\zeta,z,s)=0\operatorname{mod}\,(y,\zeta)^{4}
.
There is some
\zeta^{\prime}
of the shape

\zeta^{\prime}=\zeta+(\mathrm{unit})\cdot\nu_{3}(z,s)y=z\cdot\prod_{i}T_{i}^{e_{i}^{\prime}}+(\mathrm{unit})\cdot\nu_{3}(z,s)y,

so that the previous function becomes

\displaystyle y\Big((\mathrm{unit})\cdot y^{2}+\nu_{4}(y,\zeta^{\prime},z,s)y^{2}+(\mathrm{unit})\cdot(\zeta^{\prime})^{3}\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle y\Big((\mathrm{unit})\cdot y^{2}+(\mathrm{unit})\cdot(\zeta^{\prime})^{3}\Big),

where
\nu_{4}(y,\zeta^{\prime},z,s)=0\operatorname{mod}\,(y,\zeta^{\prime})
.

Therefore, we deduce that

\displaystyle F(x,y,z,s)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x^{2}+a(s)\prod_{i}T_{i}^{e_{i}}\cdot R_{1}(y,z,s)\cdot y^{3}

\displaystyle+a(s)\prod_{i}T_{i}^{e_{i}}\cdot R_{2}(y,z,s)\cdot y\Big(R_{3}(y,z,s)z\cdot\prod_{i}T_{i}^{e_{i}^{\prime}}+\nu_{3}(z,s)y\Big)^{3},

where
R_{1},R_{2},R_{3}
are units.
We write
\nu_{3}(z,s)=\mu_{1}(s)+z\mu_{2}(z,s)
.
Let
T^{\prime}=\prod_{i}T_{i}^{e^{\prime}_{i}}
.
By blowing up
X
at each
H_{i}
for
e_{i}^{\prime}
times as in Subsection
3.C
,
the defining function
F
becomes

\displaystyle F(x,y,z,s)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x^{2}+a(s)T^{\prime}\cdot\prod_{i}T_{i}^{e_{i}}\cdot R_{1}(T^{\prime}y,T^{\prime}z,s)\cdot y^{3}

\displaystyle+a(s)(T^{\prime})^{2}\cdot\prod_{i}T_{i}^{e_{i}}\cdot R_{2}(T^{\prime}y,T^{\prime}z,s)

\displaystyle\cdot y\Big(R_{3}(T^{\prime}y,T^{\prime}z,s)z\cdot T^{\prime}+\mu_{1}(s)y+T^{\prime}z\cdot\mu_{2}(T^{\prime}z,s)y\Big)^{3}.

The last line in the previous equation is equal to

y\Big((\mathrm{unit})\cdot z\cdot T^{\prime}+\mu_{1}(s)y\Big)^{3}.

Hence we can write

\displaystyle F(x,y,z,s)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x^{2}+a_{1}(s)\cdot Q_{1}(y,z,s)\cdot y^{3}

\displaystyle+a_{2}(s)\cdot Q_{2}(y,z,s)\cdot y\Big(Q_{3}(y,z,s)z\cdot T^{\prime}+\mu_{1}(s)y\Big)^{3},

where
Q_{1},Q_{2},Q_{3}
are units and the zero loci of
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
are both contained in
H
.
We let
Q_{4}=Q_{2}\cdot Q_{1}^{-1}
.
Then there is a unit
Q_{5}
such that
Q_{4}^{3}Q_{5}=Q_{1}
and
Q_{4}^{4}Q_{5}=Q_{2}
.
We can write

\displaystyle F(x,y,z,s)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x^{2}+a_{1}(s)\cdot Q_{5}\cdot(Q_{4}\cdot y)^{3}

\displaystyle+a_{2}(s)\cdot Q_{5}\cdot(Q_{4}\cdot y)\cdot(Q_{3}Q_{4}\cdot z\cdot T^{\prime}+\mu_{1}(s)Q_{4}\cdot y)^{3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle Q_{5}\cdot\Big(Q_{5}^{-1}x^{2}+a_{1}(s)\cdot(Q_{4}\cdot y)^{3}

\displaystyle+a_{2}(s)\cdot(Q_{4}\cdot y)\cdot(Q_{3}Q_{4}\cdot z\cdot T^{\prime}+\mu_{1}(s)Q_{4}\cdot y)^{3}\Big).

Hence, up to a change of coordinates,
X
can be defined by an equation of the shape

x^{2}+\alpha(s)y^{3}+\beta(s)y(\gamma(s)z+u(y,s)y)^{3}=0,

where
\alpha,\beta
and
\gamma
are holomorphic functions on
S
, whose zero loci are contained in
H
.
In the end, we note that if we continue blowing up
X
at the
H_{i}
’s,
then the defining equation remains the same shape.
This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

5.D.
Case of
D
-type singularities

In this subsection, we treat Proposition
5.2
in the case when
X
has
D
-type singularities at points of
S\setminus H
.

Lemma 5.9
.

Proposition
5.2
holds if
X
has
D
-type singularities at points of
S\setminus H
.

Proof.

We can assume that
X
is a hypersurface in
\mathbb{D}^{3}\times S
defined by the following equation of standard form with respect to
(S,H)
,

F(x,y,z,s)=x^{2}+F_{2}(y,z,s)+F_{3}(y,z,s)+R(y,z,s)=0,

where
F_{2}
and
F_{3}
are homogeneous in
(y,z)
of degree 2 and 3 respectively,
and
R
is a holomorphic function such that
R(y,z,s)=0\operatorname{mod}\,(y,z)^{4}
.
By Lemma
2.2
, we see that
F_{2}=0
.
Since
F
is of standard form, up to shrinking
X
and up to a linear change of the coordinates
(y,z)
,
we may assume that

F_{3}(y,z,s)=a(s)(y^{3}+b(s)y^{2}z+c(s)yz^{2}+d(s)z^{3})

such that the zero locus of
a(s)
is contained in
H
.
We blow up
X
at each
H_{i}
once (in arbitrary order), the shape
F_{3}
is unchanged.

By Lemma
2.2
,
F_{3}(y,z,s)
has either two or three distinct factors, for any
s\in S\setminus H
.
We apply Lemma
2.9
to the following cubic polynomial in
\frac{y}{z}
,

a(s)^{-1}F_{3}(\frac{y}{z},1,s)=(\frac{y}{z})^{3}+b(s)\cdot(\frac{y}{z})^{2}z+c(s)\cdot\frac{y}{z}+d(s).

Let
X_{2}\rightarrow X
be the basechange over
\overline{S}=S[\sqrt[6]{T_{1}},...,\sqrt[6]{T_{n}}]\rightarrow S
, let
\overline{S}
be the preimage of
S
in
X_{2}
, and let
\overline{H}_{i}
be the preimage of
H_{i}
for every
i=1,...,n
.
In the remainder of the proof, by abuse of notation, we will write
s\in S
for
\overline{s}\in\overline{S}
,
H=\sum_{i=1}^{n}H_{i}
for
\overline{H}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\overline{H}_{i}
,
X
for a neighborhood of
\overline{S}
in
X_{2}
,
and
F
for a defining function of
X
.
Then Lemma
2.9
implies that

F_{3}(y,z,s)=a(s)(y-p(s)z)(y-q(s)z)(y-r(s)z)

for holomorphic functions
p,q,r
on
S
.

Case 1.

We first assume that
X
has
D_{l}
-type singularities at points of
S\setminus H
with
l\geq 5
.
by Lemma
2.2
, we may assume that
q(s)=r(s)
.
Up to replacing
y
by
y+p(s)z
, we can write

F_{3}(y,z,s)=a(s)y(y-q(s)z)^{2},

such that the zero locus of
q
is contained in
H
.
In particular, it is of the shape

q(s)=T_{1}^{l_{1}}\cdots T_{n}^{ln}\cdot\overline{q}(s),

where
\overline{q}(s)
is a unit.
Up to replacing
z
be
\overline{q}(s)^{-1}\cdot z
, we may assume that

q(s)=T_{1}^{l_{1}}\cdots T_{n}^{l_{n}}.

By blowing up
X
at the
H_{i}
’s for several times as in Subsection
3.C
,
we may assume that
a(s)q(s)^{4}
divides
R(y,z,s)
,
see Remark
3.7
.
Hence we can write
R(y,z,s)=a(s)q(s)^{4}Q(y,z,s)
for some holomorphic function
Q
with
Q(y,z,s)=0\operatorname{mod}\,(y,z)^{4}
.
Then we can write

F(x,y,z,s)=x^{2}+a(s)\Big(y(y^{2}-2q(s)zy+q(s)^{2}z^{2})+q(s)^{4}\cdot Q(y,z,s)\Big)

By applying Lemma
5.3
to the function in the parenthesis after
a(s)
above,
the defining function of
X
can be written as

x^{2}+a(s)\cdot\Big(y+q(s)^{2}w(y,z,s)\Big)\Big(q(s)^{2}z^{2}-2q(s)yz+y^{2}+q(s)^{2}v(y,z,s)\Big),

where
w(y,z,s)
is divisible by
z^{2}
and
v(y,z,s)=0\operatorname{mod}\,(y,z)^{3}
.
We replace
y+q(s)^{2}w(y,z,s)
by
y
, then the second summand above becomes

\displaystyle G(y,z,s)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle a(s)\cdot y\cdot\Big(R_{1}(y,z,s)\cdot q(s)^{2}z^{2}

\displaystyle-2R_{2}(y,s)\cdot q(s)zy+y^{2}+q(s)^{2}R_{3}(y,s)\Big),

where
R_{1}
is a unit and
R_{3}(y,z)=0\operatorname{mod}\,(y)^{3}
.
Therefore, by blowing up
X
at the
H_{i}^{\prime}s
for several times as in Subsection
3.C
, we may assume that

\displaystyle G(y,z,s)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle a(s)y\cdot\Big(R_{4}(y,q(s)z,s)\cdot q(s)^{2}z^{2}

\displaystyle-2R_{2}(y,s)\cdot q(s)zy+y^{2}+q(s)^{2}R_{3}(y,s)\Big),

where
R_{4}
is a unit, see Remark
3.8
.

If we set
\zeta=q(s)z
, then by using Weierstrass preparation theorem with respect to the variable
\zeta
for the function in the parenthesis after
a(s)y
above, we can write

G(y,z,s)=(\mathrm{unit})\cdot a(s)y\cdot\left(\zeta^{2}+2R_{5}(y,s)\zeta y+R_{6}(y,s)\right),

where
R_{6}=0\operatorname{mod}\,(y)^{2}
.
It follows that we can write

G(y,z,s)=(\mathrm{unit})\cdot a(s)\cdot\Big(y\cdot(q(s)z+R_{5}(y,s)\cdot y)^{2}+R_{7}(y,s)\Big),

where
R_{7}(y,s)=0\operatorname{mod}\,(y)^{3}
.

Since
X
has
D_{l}
-type singularities at points of
S\setminus H
with
l\geq 5
,
we deduce that

R_{7}(y,s)=b(s)y^{l-1}+y^{l}\cdot c(y,s),

where the zero locus of
b
is contained in
H
.
By blowing up
X
at the
H_{i}^{\prime}s
for several times as in Subsection
3.C
,
the function
R_{7}(y,s)
can be written as

R_{7}(y,s)=b(s)\cdot R_{8}(y,s)\cdot y^{l-1},

where
R_{8}
is a unit, see Remark
3.7
.
Hence the defining function of
X
becomes

F(x,y,z,s)=x^{2}+a(s)\cdot(\mathrm{unit})\cdot\Big(y(q(s)z+R_{5}(y,s)y)^{2}+b(s)\cdot R_{8}(y,s)\cdot y^{l-1}\Big)

Let
R_{9}(y,z)
be a
(l-2)
-root of
R_{8}(y,s)
. Then we have

\displaystyle F(x,y,z,s)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x^{2}+a(s)\cdot(\mathrm{unit})\cdot\Big(y(q(s)z+R_{5}\cdot y)^{2}

\displaystyle+b(s)\cdot R_{9}^{l-2}\cdot y^{l-1}\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x^{2}+a(s)\cdot(\mathrm{unit})\cdot R_{9}^{-1}\cdot\Big(R_{9}\cdot y\cdot(q(s)z+R_{5}\cdot y)^{2}

\displaystyle+b(s)\cdot(R_{9}\cdot y)^{l-1}\Big).

Up to replacing
y
by
R_{9}(y,s)^{-1}y
,
the equation
F(x,y,z,s)=0
is equivalent to

(\mathrm{unit})\cdot x^{2}+a(s)\Big(y(q(s)z-R_{10}(y,s)y)^{2}+b(s)\cdot y^{l-1}\Big)=0.

Hence, up to a change of coordinates,
X
is now defined by

x^{2}+\alpha(s)\cdot y(\gamma(s)z+u(y,s)y)^{2}+\beta(s)y^{l-1}=0,

as in the statement of Proposition
5.2
.

Case 2.

Now we assume that
X
has
D_{4}
-type singularities at points of
S\setminus H
.
By Lemma
2.2
, and by
replacing
y
by
y+p(s)z
, we can write

F_{3}(y,z,s)=a(s)y(y-q(s)z)(y-r(s)z),

such that the zero locus of
q
and
r
are contained in
H
, and that the zero locus of
q(s)-r(s)
is also contained in
H
.
Hence, up to switching
q
and
r
, there are unit functions
\overline{q}
and
\overline{r}
such that.

q(s)=T_{1}^{l_{1}}\cdots T_{n}^{l_{n}}\cdot\overline{q}(s)\mbox{ and }r(s)=T_{1}^{l_{1}+k_{1}}\cdots T_{n}^{l_{n}+k_{n}}\cdot\overline{r}(s)

for some integers
l_{1},...,l_{n},k_{1},...,k_{n}\geq 0
.
Let
\overline{v}
be a square root of
\overline{q}\overline{r}
.
Then, by replacing
z
by
\overline{v}^{-1}z
, we may assume that
\overline{q}\cdot\overline{r}=1
.
We set
\mu(s)=T_{1}^{l_{1}}\cdots T_{n}^{l_{n}}
and
\nu(s)=T_{1}^{k_{1}}\cdots T_{n}^{k_{n}}
.
Then we have

q(s)r(s)=T_{1}^{2l_{1}+k_{1}}\cdots T_{n}^{2l_{n}+k_{n}}=\mu(s)^{2}\cdot\nu(s).

We write
(y-q(s)z)(y-r(s)z)=y^{2}+\xi(s)zy+\eta(s)z^{2}
.
That is,

\eta(s)=q(s)r(s)=\mu(s)^{2}\cdot\nu(s)\mbox{ and }\xi(s)=q(s)+r(s)=(\overline{q}(s)+\overline{r}(s)\nu(s))\cdot\mu(s).

By blowing up
X
at the
H_{i}^{\prime}s
for several times as Subsection
3.C
,
we may assume that
a(s)\eta(s)^{2}
divides
R(y,z,s)
, see Remark
3.7
.
Then we have

F(x,y,z,s)=x^{2}+a(s)\Big(y(y^{2}+\xi(s)zy+\eta(s)z^{2})+\eta(s)^{2}Q(y,z,s)\Big),

where
Q(y,z,s)=0\operatorname{mod}\,(y,z)^{4}
.
By applying Lemma
5.3
to the function in the parenthesis after
a(s)
above,
the defining function of
X
can be written as

x^{2}+a(s)\cdot\Big(y+\eta(s)w(y,z,s)\Big)\Big(\eta(s)z^{2}-2\xi(s)yz+y^{2}+\eta(s)v(y,z,s)\Big),

where
w(y,z,s)
is divisible by
z^{2}
and
v(y,z,s)=0\operatorname{mod}\,(y,z)^{3}
.
We replace
y
by
y-\eta(s)w(y,z,s)
, then the second summand above becomes

\displaystyle G(y,z,s)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle a(s)y\cdot\Big(R_{1}(y,z,s)\cdot\eta(s)z^{2}

\displaystyle-2\xi(s)zy+R_{2}(y,z,s)y^{2}\Big),

where
