R_{1}
is a unit.
Recall that
\eta(s)=\mu(s)^{2}\nu(s)
and that
\xi(s)
is divisible by
\mu(s)
.
Then we have

\displaystyle G(y,z,s)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\nu(s)}\cdot a(s)y\cdot\Big(R_{1}(y,z,s)\cdot\mu(s)^{2}\nu(s)^{2}z^{2}

\displaystyle-2\cdot\frac{\xi(s)}{\mu(s)}\cdot\mu(s)\nu(s)z\cdot y+\nu(s)R_{2}(y,z,s)y^{2}\Big).

By blowing up
X
at the
H_{i}^{\prime}s
for several times as in Subsection
3.C
,
we may assume that the function in the parenthesis after
a(s)y
above
is a function of
(y,\mu(s)\nu(s)z,s)
, see Remark
3.8
.
In other words, we can assume that

\displaystyle G(y,z,s)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\nu(s)}\cdot a(s)y\cdot\Big(R_{3}(y,\mu(s)\nu(s)z,s)\cdot\mu(s)^{2}\nu(s)^{2}z^{2}

\displaystyle+2R_{4}(s)\cdot\mu(s)\nu(s)z\cdot y+R_{5}(y,\mu(s)\nu(s)z,s)\nu(s)\cdot y^{2}\Big),

where
R_{3}
is a unit.

We remark that if we blow up
X
at the
H_{i}^{\prime}
s for sufficiently many times, we can assume that
a(s)
is divisible by
\nu(s)
.
Indeed, if we blow up
X
at
H_{i}
, then
a(s)
becomes
T_{i}\cdot a(s)
but
\nu(s)
remains the same.
By abuse of notation, we denote
\frac{1}{\nu(s)}\cdot a(s)
by
a(s)
in the following computations.

If we set
\zeta=\mu(s)\nu(s)z
, then by applying Weierstrass preparation theorem with respect to the variable
\zeta
to the function in the parenthesis after
a(s)y
above, we can write

G(y,z,s)=(\mathrm{unit})\cdot a(s)y\cdot\Big(\zeta^{2}+2R_{6}(y,s)\zeta y+R_{7}(y,s)\Big),

where
R_{7}=0\operatorname{mod}\,(y)^{2}
.
It follows that we can write

G(y,z,s)=(\mathrm{unit})\cdot a(s)\cdot\Big(y\cdot\big(\mu(s)\nu(s)z+R_{6}(y,s)y\big)^{2}+R_{8}(y,s)\Big),

where
R_{8}(y,z)=0\operatorname{mod}\,(y)^{3}
.
Since
X
has
D_{4}
-type singularities at points of
S\setminus H
,
we deduce that

R_{8}(y,s)=b(s)y^{3}+y^{4}c(y,s),

where the zero locus of
b
is contained in
H
.
By blowing up
X
at the
H_{i}
’s for several times as in Subsection
3.C
,
the function
R_{8}(y,s)
can be written as

R_{8}(y,s)=b(s)\cdot(\mathrm{unit})\cdot y^{3},

see Remark
3.7
.
Then we can write

F(x,y,z,s)=x^{2}+(\mathrm{unit})\cdot a(s)\cdot y\cdot\big(\mu(s)\nu(s)z+R_{8}(y,s)y\big)^{2}+(\mathrm{unit})\cdot a(s)b(s)\cdot y^{3}.

Now as in the proof for the Case 1,
we deduce that
X
can be defined by

x^{2}+\alpha(s)\cdot y(\gamma(s)z+u(y,s)y)^{2}+\beta(s)y^{3}=0,

as in Proposition
5.2
.
In the end, we note that if we continue blowing up
X
at some
H_{i}
, then the defining equation remains the same shape.
This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Remark 5.10
.

If we let
\theta\colon X\rightarrow S
be the projection induced by the canonical one from
\mathbb{D}^{3}\times S
to
S
, then we can regard
T_{i}
as a holomorphic function on
X_{1}
by pulling it back
via

\theta\circ f_{1}\colon X_{1}\rightarrow S
.
Then in a neighborhood of the strict transform
(f_{1}^{-1})_{*}S
of
S
in
X_{1}
, the Cartier divisor defined by
T_{i}=0
is the
f_{1}
-exceptional divisor defined by
f_{1}^{-1}\mathcal{I}({H_{i}})\cdot\mathcal{O}_{X_{1}}
, where
\mathcal{I}({H_{i}})
is the ideal sheaf of
H_{i}
in
X
, see Remark
3.5
.
Furthermore, we have
X_{2}=X_{1}[\sqrt[6]{T_{1}},...,\sqrt[6]{T_{n}}]
.

Remark 5.11
.

The objective that we first construct
f_{1}\colon X_{1}\rightarrow X
is to make the finite cover
f_{2}\colon X_{2}\rightarrow X_{1}
functorial.
For the holomorphic function
T_{i}
on
S
, there is no canonical way to make it a holomorphic function on
X
.
And the Cartier divisors in
X
defined by
T_{i}=0
depend on the choice of coordinates in Setup
5.1
.
However, in a neighborhood
V
of the strict transform
(f_{1}^{-1})_{*}S
of
S
in
X_{1}
, the Cartier divisor defined by
T_{i}=0
is the
f_{1}
-exceptional divisor defined by
f_{1}^{-1}\mathcal{I}({H_{i}})\cdot\mathcal{O}_{X_{1}}
.
In particular, this Cartier divisor is intrinsic.
To see this more explicitly, we assume that

\varphi\colon(x,y,z,s)\mapstochar\rightarrow(x^{\prime},y^{\prime},z^{\prime},s^{\prime})

is a change of coordinates, and we regard
x^{\prime},y^{\prime},z^{\prime},s^{\prime}
as holomorphic vector-valued functions on
(x,y,z,s)
.
Then
s^{\prime}=s+P
with
P=0\operatorname{mod}\,(x,y,z)
.
Let
T=T_{1}\cdots T_{n}
.
Then
f_{1}
can be written in coordinates as

f_{1}\colon(x_{1},y_{1},z_{1},s)\mapstochar\rightarrow(T(s)x_{1},T(s)y_{1},T(s)z_{1},s)=(x,y,z,s)

and

f_{1}\colon(x_{1}^{\prime},y_{1}^{\prime},z_{1}^{\prime},s^{\prime})\mapstochar\rightarrow(T(s^{\prime})x_{1}^{\prime},T(s^{\prime})y_{1}^{\prime},T(s^{\prime})z_{1}^{\prime},s^{\prime})=(x^{\prime},y^{\prime},z^{\prime},s^{\prime})

As a consequence, the change of coordinates
\varphi_{1}\colon(x_{1},y_{1},z_{1},s)\mapstochar\rightarrow(x_{1}^{\prime},y_{1}^{\prime},z_{1}^{\prime},s^{\prime})
satisfies

s^{\prime}(x_{1},y_{1},z_{1},s)=s+P(T(s)x_{1},T(s)y_{1},T(s)z_{1},s)

Since
P(x,y,z,s)=0\operatorname{mod}\,(x,y,z)
, there exists some vector-valued holomorphic function
Q
such that

P(T(s)x_{1},T(s)y_{1},T(s)z_{1},s)=T(s)Q(x_{1},y_{1},z_{1},s)
and that
Q(x_{1},y_{1},z_{1},s)=0\operatorname{mod}\,(x_{1},y_{1},z_{1})
.
Then

T_{i}(x_{1}^{\prime},y_{1}^{\prime},z_{1}^{\prime},s^{\prime})=T_{i}(s^{\prime})=T_{i}(s+T(s)Q(x_{1},y_{1},z_{1},s)).

It follows that

\displaystyle T_{i}(x_{1}^{\prime},y_{1}^{\prime},z_{1}^{\prime},s^{\prime})-T_{i}(x_{1},y_{1},z_{1},s)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle T_{i}(s+T(s)Q(x_{1},y_{1},z_{1},s))-T_{i}(s)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle T(s)\cdot R(x_{1},y_{1},z_{1},s),

such that
R(x_{1},y_{1},z_{1},s)=0\operatorname{mod}\,(x_{1},y_{1},z_{1})
.
Since
T_{i}(s)
divides
T(s)
, up to shrinking
V
, we have

T_{i}(x_{1}^{\prime},y_{1}^{\prime},z_{1}^{\prime},s^{\prime})=(\mathrm{unit})\cdot T_{i}(x_{1},y_{1},z_{1},s).

As a result, by Lemma
2.5
,
the finite morphism
f_{2}\colon X_{2}\rightarrow X_{1}
constructed
via
two choices of coordinates
in Setup
5.1
are the same on
V
.

6.
Local construction of covering spaces

In this section, we will construct a finite Galois cover for a hypersurface
X
defined by some equation as in Proposition
5.2
, so that the covering space is smooth over
S\setminus H
.

We fix the following convention for this section.
Assume that
M
is a path-connected topological space and
m\in M
is a point.
We recall that
\pi_{1}(M,m)
, the fundamental group of
M
with basepoint
m
,
is the group of homotopy classes of loops in
M
with basepoint
m
.
The fundamental group of
M
,
\pi_{1}(M)
, is the Galois group of the universal cover of
M
.
There is an isomorphism from
\pi_{1}(M,m)
to
\pi_{1}(M)
.
We will work with fundamental groups without basepoint in this section.
By Galois theory, there is a correspondence between normal subgroups of
\pi_{1}(M)
and isomorphism classes of Galois covers over
M
.

If
\overline{m}\in M
is another point, then there are isomorphisms
\pi_{1}(M,m)\cong\pi_{1}(M,\overline{m})
induced by the paths joining
m
and
\overline{m}
.
These isomorphisms are in bijection with the inner automorphisms of
\pi_{1}(M,\overline{m}).

Now we assume that
f\colon M\rightarrow M^{\prime}
is a continuous map between path-connected topological spaces. If
m^{\prime}=f(m)
, then there is a natural morphism

f_{m,*}\colon\pi_{1}(M,m)\rightarrow\pi_{1}(M^{\prime},m^{\prime}),

which induces a morphism of fundamental groups
\pi_{1}(M)\rightarrow\pi_{1}(M^{\prime})
.
The conjugacy class of
\pi_{1}(M)\rightarrow\pi_{1}(M^{\prime})
is independent of the choice of basepoints.
By abuse of notation, we will call this conjugacy class the natural morphism induced by
f
, and denote it by

f_{*}\colon\pi_{1}(M)\rightarrow\pi_{1}(M^{\prime}).

By the image of
f_{*}
, we refer to the conjugacy class of subgroups in
\pi_{1}(M^{\prime})
induced by
f_{m,*}(\pi_{1}(M,m))
.
When it is the conjugacy class of a normal subgroup, then the quotient of
\pi_{1}(M^{\prime})
by the image of
f_{*}
is well-defined.
We say that
f_{*}
is surjective if its image is the conjugacy class of
\pi_{1}(M^{\prime})
.
For any normal subgroup
H
of
\pi_{1}(M^{\prime})
, its preimage in
\pi_{1}(M)
is a well-defined normal subgroup, and we denote it by
(f_{*})^{-1}(H)
.
In particular, we can define the kernel of
f_{*}
.
We say that
f_{*}
is injective if its kernel is the trivial subgroup of
\pi_{1}(M)
, and that
f_{*}
is an isomorphism if it is both surjective and injective.

6.A.
Preparatory lemmas

In this part, we will prove some properties on varieties of the shape
Y\times\mathbb{D}^{n}
, where
Y
is a Du Val singularity.
We start with two elementary results.

Lemma 6.1
.

Let
U\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{m}
and
S\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{n}
be open neighborhoods of the origins,
and let
f\colon U\times S\rightarrow S
be a morphism such that

f(\mathbf{0}_{m},\mathbf{0}_{n})=\mathbf{0}_{n}.

Then there are open neighborhoods
W\subseteq U
and
T\subseteq S
of the origins, such that

f|_{W\times T}
is a continuous deformation equivalence to the projection
p
onto
T
, with values contained in
S
.
In other words, there is a continuous function

H(u,s,\mu)\colon W\times T\times[0,1]\rightarrow S,

such that
H(u,s,0)=s
and
H(u,s,1)=f(u,s)
.

Proof.

There is a real number
r>0
such that

S
contains the ball in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
of radius
2r>0
centered at the origin.
We can define the function
g(u,s)=f(u,s)-s
on
U\times S
,
which takes values in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
.
Then the assumption implies that
g(\mathbf{0}_{m},\mathbf{0}_{n})=\mathbf{0}_{n}.

Thus there are open neighborhoods
W\subseteq U
and
T\subseteq S
of the origins,
such that
|g(u,s)|<r
for
(u,s)\in W\times T
.
We can choose
T
so that it is contained in the ball in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
of radius
r
centered at the origin.
We define the function
H(u,s,\mu)=s+\mu g(u,s)
on
W\times T\times[0,1]
.
Then
H
has values in
S
,
and it induces a deformation equivalence from
f|_{W\times T}
to
p
.
This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Lemma 6.2
.

Let
M
and
M^{\prime}
be path-connected topological spaces,
and let
H(m,\mu)
be a continuous map from
M\times[0,1]
to
M^{\prime}
.
If we denote by
\varphi_{0}
and
\varphi_{1}
the restriction of
H
on
M\times\{0\}
and
on
M\times\{1\}
respectively,
then the morphisms of fundamental groups

(\varphi_{0})_{*},(\varphi_{1})_{*}\colon\pi_{1}(M)\rightarrow\pi_{1}(M^{\prime})

have the same image.

Proof.

For
i=0,1
,
we let
\iota_{i}\colon M\rightarrow M\times[0,1]
be the continuous map
sending a point
m\in M
to
(m,i)
.
Then both
\iota_{0}
and
\iota_{1}
induce an isomorphism from
\pi_{1}(M)
to
\pi_{1}(M\times I)
.
Since
\varphi_{i}=H\circ\iota_{i}
for
i=0,1
, the images of
(\varphi_{0})_{*}
and
(\varphi_{1})_{*}
are both equal to the image of
H_{*}\colon\pi_{1}(M\times I)\rightarrow\pi_{1}(M^{\prime})
.
∎

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
Y\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{3}
be the hypersurface defined by
F(x,y,z)=0
, such that
F
is one of the following functions,

\displaystyle F(x,y,z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x^{2}+y^{2}+z^{l},\ l\geq 2,

\displaystyle F(x,y,z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x^{2}+yz^{2}+y^{l},\ l\geq 3,

\displaystyle F(x,y,z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x^{2}+y^{3}+yz^{3},

\displaystyle F(x,y,z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x^{2}+y^{3}+z^{l},l=4,5,

where
l
is a positive integer.
Let
\check{S}
be a contractible open neighborhood of the origin in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
and let
X=Y\times\check{S}
.
Assume that
(T_{1},...,T_{n})
is a coordinates system of
\mathbb{C}^{n}
.
Let
T=T_{1}\cdots T_{k}
for some integer
0\leq k\leq n
, and let
H=\{T=0\}
.
We set
\check{S}^{\circ}=\check{S}\setminus H
.
Then for any positive integer
N_{0}
, there exists positive integers
p,q,r\geq N_{0}
such that the morphism

\varphi\colon(x,y,z,s)\mapstochar\rightarrow(T^{p}x,T^{q}y,T^{r}z,s)

induces an automorphism of
Y\times\check{S}^{\circ}
over
\check{S}^{\circ}
.

Proof.

We only treat the case when
Y\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{3}
is defined by

0=F(x,y,z)=x^{2}+yz^{2}+y^{l}.

The other cases are similar.
In order that the map
\varphi
is well-defined from
Y\times\check{S}^{\circ}
to itself,
it is sufficient that there is some positive integer
d
, so that

(T^{p}x)^{2}+(T^{q}y)(T^{r}z)^{2}+(T^{q}y)^{l}=T^{d}(x^{2}+yz^{2}+y^{l}).

It is equivalent to the system

2p=q+2r=lq.

There three unknowns and two equations, hence the system is indeterminate and admits infinitely many integer solutions.
Furthermore, since
l\geq 2
, we see that the solutions for
q,r
tend to
+\infty
if
p
tends to
+\infty
.
Hence there are integers
p,q,r\geq N_{0}
such that
\varphi
is well-defined.
Since
T
is nowhere vanishing on
\check{S}^{\circ}
, we deduce that
\varphi
is an automorphism of
Y\times\check{S}^{\circ}
over
\check{S}^{\circ}
.
∎

With the notation in the previous lemma, we note that there is a finite subgroup
\Gamma
of
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
such that
Y=\mathbb{C}^{2}/\Gamma
, see
[
Lam86
, Section II.8]
.
In particular, for any open subset
U\subseteq Y
containing
\mathbf{0}_{3}
,
there is an open subset
W\subseteq U
such that the natural morphism from

\pi_{1}(W_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}})
to
\pi_{1}(Y_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}})
is isomorphic.

Lemma 6.4
.

With the notation in Lemma
6.3
,
Let
U\subseteq Y
be an open neighborhood of
\mathbf{0}_{3}
such that the natural morphism of fundamental groups
\pi_{1}(U_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}})\rightarrow\pi_{1}(Y_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}})
is an isomorphism.
Let
S\subseteq\check{S}
be an open subset,
such that
S
is also isomorphic to a contractible open neighborhood of the origin in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
, and that
H|_{S}
is isomorphic to the union of coordinates hyperplanes.
Let
S^{\circ}=S\setminus H
.

Let
\varphi\colon U\times S^{\circ}\rightarrow Y\times\check{S}^{\circ}
be an open embedding,
whose restriction on
\{\mathbf{0}_{3}\}\times S^{\circ}
is the identity map.
Then the induced morphism
\varphi_{*}
of fundamental groups from
\pi_{1}(U_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}\times S^{\circ})
to
\pi_{1}(Y_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}\times\check{S}^{\circ})
is injective.
Furthermore, its image is equal to the image of
\pi_{1}(Y_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}\times S^{\circ})

induced by the natural inclusion.
In particular, if
k^{\prime}
is the number of irreducible components of
H|_{S}
,
then we have

\pi_{1}(Y_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}\times\check{S}^{\circ})/\varphi_{*}(\pi_{1}(U_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}\times S^{\circ}))\cong\mathbb{Z}^{k-k^{\prime}}.

Proof.

Let
o\in S^{\circ}
, let
u\in U_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}
and let
V=\{u\}\times S^{\circ}\subseteq U_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}\times S^{\circ}
.
Then we can write
\pi_{1}(U_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}\times S^{\circ})=H_{1}\times G_{1}
, where

G_{1}=\pi_{1}(V)\cong\pi_{1}(S^{\circ})\cong\mathbb{Z}^{k^{\prime}},\ H_{1}=\pi_{1}(U_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}\times\{o\})\cong\pi_{1}(U_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}).

Similarly, we can write
\pi_{1}(Y_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}\times S^{\circ})=H_{2}\times G_{2}
, where

G_{2}\cong\pi_{1}(\check{S}^{\circ})\cong\mathbb{Z}^{k}\mbox{ and }H_{2}=\pi_{1}(Y_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}\times\{o\})\cong\pi_{1}(Y_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}).

Since
Y
is a hypersurface in
\mathbb{C}^{3}
, locally around
(\mathbf{0}_{3},o)\in U\times S^{\circ}
, the morphism
\varphi
can be written in coordinates as

\varphi\colon(x,y,z,s)\mapstochar\rightarrow(x^{\prime},y^{\prime},z^{\prime},s^{\prime})

and we can regard
x^{\prime},y^{\prime},z^{\prime},s^{\prime}
as vector-valued functions in
(x,y,z,s)
.
The assumption implies that
s^{\prime}(0,0,0,s)=s
.
We let

\psi\colon(x,y,z,s)\mapstochar\rightarrow(x,y,z,s^{\prime})

be a morphism defined in neighborhoods of
(\mathbf{0}_{3},o)\in U\times S^{\circ}
.
By the implicit function theorem, there is an open neighborhood
\overline{W}
of
\mathbf{0}_{3}
in
U
, and an open neighborhood
\overline{T}
of
o\in S^{\circ}
,
such that
\psi
is an isomorphism from
\overline{W}\times\overline{T}
to its image
Z
inside
U\times\check{S}^{\circ}
.
We note that
Z
is an open neighborhood of
(\mathbf{0}_{3},o)
in
U\times\check{S}^{\circ}
.

By Lemma
6.1
,
there is an open neighborhood
W
of
\mathbf{0}_{3}
in
\overline{W}
,
there are open neighborhoods
T\subseteq T^{\prime}
of
o
in
\overline{T}
,
such that the restriction of
s^{\prime}
on
W\times T
is deformation equivalent to the projection function
(x,y,z,s)\mapstochar\rightarrow s
, with values inside
T^{\prime}
.
Up to shrinking
W
and
T^{\prime}
, we may assume that
W\times T^{\prime}\subseteq Z
.
As a consequence, the image
\psi(W_{sm}\times\{o\})
is deformation equivalent to
W_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}\times\{o\}
inside
Z_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}=Z\setminus(\{\mathbf{0}_{3}\}\times S^{\circ})
.
Then by Lemma
6.2
, the image of

\psi_{*}\colon\pi_{1}(W_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}\times\{o\})\rightarrow\pi_{1}(Z_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}})

is equal to the one of
\pi_{1}(W_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}\times\{o\})
induced by the natural inclusion

W_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}\times\{o\}\rightarrow Z_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}.

Moreover, we can choose
W
so that the natural morphism

\pi_{1}(W_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}})\rightarrow\pi_{1}(U_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}})
is isomorphic.

Let
\eta\colon Z\rightarrow Y\times\check{S}^{\circ}
be the morphism defined by
\varphi\circ\psi^{-1}
,
which is an open embedding.
Then
\eta
can be written in coordinates as

\eta\colon(x,y,z,s^{\prime})\mapstochar\rightarrow(x^{\prime},y^{\prime},z^{\prime},s^{\prime}).

We see that the image
\eta(W\times\{o\})
is a neighborhood of
(\mathbf{0}_{3},o)
in the fiber
Y\times\{o\}
.
Since
\pi_{1}(Y_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}})
can be generated by loops in an arbitrarily small neighborhood of
\mathbf{0}_{3}\in Y
,
the morphism
\eta_{*}
of fundamental groups from
\pi_{1}(W_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}\times\{o\})
to

H_{2}=\pi_{1}(Y_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}\times\{o\})
is surjective.
Since both groups are finite of the same order, we deduce that

\eta_{*}\colon\pi_{1}(W_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}\times\{o\})\rightarrow H_{2}

is an isomorphism.
Since
H_{2}
is a normal subgroup of
\pi_{1}(Y_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}\times\check{S}^{\circ})
,
we conclude with the previous paragraph that

\varphi_{*}=(\eta\circ\psi)_{*}\colon\pi_{1}(W_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}\times\{o\})\rightarrow\pi_{1}(Y_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}\times\check{S}^{\circ})

induces an isomorphism from
\pi_{1}(W_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}\times\{o\})
to
H_{2}
.
This implies that the morphism
\varphi_{*}\colon\pi_{1}(U_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}\times S^{\circ})\rightarrow\pi_{1}(Y_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}\times\check{S}^{\circ})

induces an isomorphism from
H_{1}
to
H_{2}
.

Since
u
is connected to
\mathbf{0}_{3}
by path in
U
, we see that
V
is deformation equivalent to
\{\mathbf{0}_{3}\}\times S^{\circ}
inside
U\times S^{\circ}
.
Thus
\varphi(V)
is deformation equivalent to
\{\mathbf{0}_{3}\}\times S^{\circ}
inside
Y\times\check{S}^{\circ}
.
Hence by Lemma
6.2
,
if
q\colon Y\times\check{S}^{\circ}\rightarrow\check{S}^{\circ}
is the natural projection,
then
q\circ\varphi
induces an isomorphism from
\pi_{1}(V)
to
the image of
\pi_{1}(S^{\circ})
in
\pi_{1}(\check{S}^{\circ})
.
This implies that
\varphi_{*}
induces an injective map on
G_{1}
.
Since
G_{1}
is a free Abelian group, we deduce that the intersection
\varphi_{*}(G_{1})\cap\varphi_{*}(H_{1})
is trivial.
Hence
\varphi_{*}
is injective.
Finally, we notice that the image of
\varphi_{*}
is equal to the preiamge of
\pi_{1}(S^{\circ})
under the morphism

q_{*}\colon\pi_{1}(Y_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}\times\check{S}^{\circ})\rightarrow\pi_{1}(\check{S}^{\circ}),

which is equal to the image of
\pi_{1}(Y_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}\times S^{\circ})
inside
\pi_{1}(Y_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}\times\check{S}^{\circ})
.
This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

With the notation above, we remark that the fundamental group
\pi_{1}(Y_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}\times\check{S}^{\circ})
is isomorphic to
\pi_{1}(Y_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}})\times\mathbb{Z}^{k}
.
For this kind of groups, we construct a canonical subgroup (which is
N
) in the following lemma.
After the Galois theory, it induces a canonical covering space of
Y_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}\times\check{S}^{\circ}
.

Lemma 6.5
.

Let
E=H\times G
be a group, where
H\subseteq E
be a finite subgroup
and
G\subseteq E
is a subgroup isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}^{k}
for some integer
k\geq 0
.
Let
N
be the intersection of all complements of
H
in
E
.
Then
N
is of finite index in
E
, and is invariant under any automorphism of
E
.
Furthermore, let
E^{\prime}\subseteq E
be a normal subgroup such that
E/E^{\prime}\cong\mathbb{Z}^{l}
for some integer
0\leq l\leq k
.
Then
H\subseteq E
.
If
N^{\prime}
is the intersection of all complements of
H
in
E^{\prime}
,
then
N\cap E^{\prime}=N^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Since
E
is finitely generated, for any fixed number
i
,
there are only finitely many subgroups of
E
of index
i
.
Hence there are only finitely many complements of
H
in
E
.
It follows that
N
is of finite index in
E
.
Since
H
is equal to the set of all elements of finite order in
E
,
it is invariant under any automorphisms of
E
.
Hence so is
N
.

For the second part of the lemma,
since
E/E^{\prime}
is a free Abelian group and
H
is finite,
we must have
H\subseteq E^{\prime}
.
On the one hand, we assume that
K
is a complement of
H
in
E
.
Since
H\subseteq E^{\prime}
, we deduce that
K\cap E^{\prime}
is a complement of
H
in
E^{\prime}
.
Hence
N^{\prime}\subseteq N\cap E^{\prime}
.

On the other hand, the quotient map
E\rightarrow E/E^{\prime}
admits a splitting
E/E^{\prime}\rightarrow E
.
Let
J\subseteq E
be its image.
Then
E=JE^{\prime}
and
J\cap E^{\prime}=\{e\}
, where
e
is the neutral element of
E
.
Let
K^{\prime}
be a complement of
H
in
E^{\prime}
.
Then
K:=JK^{\prime}
is a complement of
H
in
E
.
Furthermore,
K\cap E^{\prime}=K^{\prime}
.
It follows that
N\cap E^{\prime}\subseteq N^{\prime}
.
This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

6.B.
Construction of covering spaces

In the remainder of this section, we work with the following setup.
Our goal is to prove Proposition
6.7
.

Setup 6.6
.

We fix the following notation for the remainder of this section.

(1)

We consider the data
X
,
\Delta
,
\check{S}
,
H
and
G
introduced as follows.
Let
X
be a complex analytic variety and let
\check{S}\subseteq X_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sing}}}
be an irreducible component of codimension
2
.
Let
\Delta
be a reduced divisor in
X
.
There is a finite group
G
acting on
X
such that
\check{S}
and
\Delta
are
G
-invariant.
The reduced pair
(\check{S},H)
is snc.
If
H_{1},\cdots,H_{k}
are the irreducible components of
H
, then each
H_{i}
is
G
-invariant.
Assume that
X
is an open subset of the hyperplane
M
in
\mathbb{D}^{3}\times\check{S}
defined by an equation
F(x,y,z,s)=0
, where
(x,y,z)
are coordinates of
\mathbb{D}^{3}
and
s\in S
is a point.
Assume that
F
is of one of the following shapes (as in Proposition
5.2
),

\displaystyle F(x,y,z,s)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x^{2}+\alpha(s)y^{2}+\beta(s)z^{l},\ l\geq 2,

\displaystyle F(x,y,z,s)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x^{2}+\alpha(s)y(\gamma(s)z+u(y,s)y)^{2}+\beta(s)y^{l},\ l\geq 3,

\displaystyle F(x,y,z,s)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x^{2}+\alpha(s)y^{3}+\beta(s)\cdot y(\gamma(s)z+u(y,s)y)^{3},

\displaystyle F(x,y,z,s)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x^{2}+\alpha(s)y^{3}+\beta(s)\cdot(\gamma(s)z+u(y,s)y)^{l},\ l=4,5,

where
l
is an integer,
\alpha(s)
,
\beta(s)
and
\gamma(s)
are holomorphic functions on
\check{S}
, whose zero loci are contained in
H
,
and
u(y,s)
is a holomorphic function defined on
\mathbb{D}\times\check{S}
.
The composite inclusion
\check{S}\subseteq X\subseteq\mathbb{D}^{3}\times\check{S}
is identified with
\{\mathbf{0}_{3}\}\times\check{S}\subseteq\mathbb{D}^{3}\times\check{S}
.
Furthermore, if
\theta\colon M\rightarrow\check{S}
is the projection sending
(x,y,z,s)
to
s
, then
\Delta=\theta^{-1}(H)\cap X
.
Let
\check{S}^{\circ}=\check{S}\setminus H
,
M^{\circ}=\theta^{-1}(\check{S}^{\circ})
, and
X^{\circ}=\theta^{-1}(\check{S}^{\circ})\cap X=X\setminus\Delta
.

(2)

The vanishing orders of
\alpha(s)
and
\beta(s)
along each
H_{i}
are divisible by 6.

(3)

Let
o_{S}\in\check{S}
be any point.
We will work in a neighborhood of
o_{S}
in
X
.
Up to shrinking
X
around
o_{S}
, up to replacing
G
by the stabilizer of
o_{S}
,
and up to reducing the number
k
,
we assume that
\check{S}
an open neighborhood of the origin
\mathbf{0}_{n}=o_{S}
of
\mathbb{C}^{n}

contained in a polydisc of radius smaller than 1.
Since
\check{S}
is smooth, the action of
G
on the germ
(o_{S}\in\check{S})
is isomorphic to a linear action (see
[
Car54
, Lemme 1]
or
[
Kaw88
, Lemma 9.9]
).
Therefore, we may assume that
\check{S}
is stable under the scaling with any positive factor less than 1.
In particular, it is contractible.
We can also assume that there is a coordinates system
(T_{1},...,T_{n})
on
\check{S}
,
such that
H_{i}=\{T_{i}=0\}
, with
i=1,...,k
.
Let
T=T_{1}\cdots T_{k}
.

(4)

Let
S=\frac{1}{2}\check{S}
be the scaling of
\check{S}
with factor
\frac{1}{2}
.
Then
S
is a
G
-invariant open neighborhood of
o_{S}
and the closure of
S
in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
is contained in
\check{S}
.
In particular, there is a disc
\mathbb{D}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathbb{D}
such that

M^{\prime}:=((\mathbb{D}^{\prime})^{3}\times S)\cap M\subseteq X\subseteq M.

Furthermore, every holomorphic function on
\check{S}
is bounded on
S
.
Let
S^{\circ}=S\setminus H
.

(5)

Let
F^{\prime}(x,y,z)
be the polynomial function obtained by replacing
\alpha,\beta,\gamma
with 1 and replacing
u
with 0 in the expression of
F
.
We denote by
Y
the hypersurface in
\mathbb{C}^{3}
defined by
F^{\prime}=0
.
Then
Y
has an isolated Du Val singularity at
\mathbf{0}_{3}
.
Let
U\subseteq Y
be a bounded open neighborhood of
\mathbf{0}_{3}
,
such that the natural morphism of fundamental groups

\pi_{1}(U_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}})\rightarrow\pi_{1}(Y_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}})

is isomorphic.

The open subset
M^{\prime}
in the item (4) of the previous setup plays an important role in our proof of Proposition
6.7
.
The reason that we work with two neighborhood
S
and
\check{S}
is following one.
The projection
\theta\colon X\rightarrow\check{S}
is not
G
-invariant in general.
As a consequence,
X
may not contain an open subset of the shape
((\mathbb{D}^{\prime})^{3}\times\check{S})\cap M
.

Proposition 6.7
.

With the notation of Setup
6.6
,
there is an integer
N\geq 0
,
such that the following properties hold for any integer
N^{\prime}\geq N
.
Let
\rho\colon\widehat{X}\rightarrow X
be the blowup of
X
at the
H_{i}
’s as in Subsection
3.C
,
such that there are
6N^{\prime}
blowups whose centers are
H_{i}
for all
i
.
Particularly, there is a natural action of
G
on
\widehat{X}
so that
\rho
is
G
-equivariant.
Let
\widehat{S}
be the strict transform of
S
in
\widehat{X}
,
let
\widehat{H}\subseteq\widehat{S}
be the preimage of
H
,
and let
\widehat{X}^{\circ}=\rho^{-1}(X^{\circ})=\widehat{X}\setminus\rho^{-1}(\Delta)
.

Then there is an open neighborhood
W
of
\widehat{S}
and
a finite cover
\nu\colon\overline{W}\rightarrow W
, which satisfy the following properties.

(1)

\nu
is quasi-étale and Galois, over the open subset
W^{\circ}:=W\cap\widehat{X}^{\circ}=W\setminus\rho^{-1}(\Delta)
.

(2)

\overline{W}
is normal.

(3)

\overline{W}
is smooth over
W^{\circ}
.

(4)

W
is
G
-invariant,
and the automorphisms of
W
over
W/G
can be lifted to automorphisms of
\overline{W}
.

(5)

The finite morphism
\nu
is functorial in the following sense.
For any point
\hat{o}\in W
, let
o=\rho(\hat{o})\in X
.
Assume that there is an open neighborhood
W^{\prime}
of
\hat{o}
in
W
,
such that there is a finite morphism
\nu^{\prime}\colon\overline{W}^{\prime}\rightarrow W^{\prime}
,
which is constructed by the same method on
a neighborhood
X^{\prime}
of
\rho(W^{\prime})
in
X
.
Then there is an open neighborhood
W^{\prime\prime}
of
\hat{o}
in
W^{\prime}
,
such that every connected component of
\nu^{-1}(W^{\prime\prime})
is isomorphic to every connected component
(\nu^{\prime})^{-1}(W^{\prime\prime})
.

We will apply this local construction on orbifold charts of some complex analytic orbispaces.
The item (5) above is to ensure that the constructions are compatible along the overlaps.
To explain the assumption on the finite morphism
\nu^{\prime}\colon\overline{W}^{\prime}\rightarrow W^{\prime}
in this item,
we first illustrate the construction of the finite morphism
\nu\colon\overline{W}\rightarrow W
.
Initially, we construct an open embedding
X^{\circ}\rightarrow Y\times\check{S}^{\circ}
,
such that its restriction on
\check{S}^{\circ}
is the identity map onto
\{\mathbf{0}_{3}\}\times\check{S}^{\circ}
.
We note that
\check{S}\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{n}
, and
\check{S}^{\circ}\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{n}\setminus H
, where we still denote by
H
its Zariski closure in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
,
which is again the union of some coordinate hyperplanes.
Let
Z\rightarrow Y_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}\times\check{S}^{\circ}
be the étale finite morphism induced by the canonical subgroup in Lemma
6.5
of the fundamental group
\pi_{1}(Y_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}\times\check{S}^{\circ})
.
Then we construct
\overline{W}\rightarrow W
as the unique finite morphism induced by the fiber product
W^{\circ}_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}\times_{X^{\circ}}Z
,
see Theorem
2.4
.
Now for the morphism
\nu\colon\overline{W}^{\prime}\rightarrow W^{\prime}
in the item (5),
the assumption particularly implies that there is an open embedding

X^{\prime}\cap X^{\circ}\rightarrow Y\times(\mathbb{C}^{n}\setminus H^{\prime})
,
where
H^{\prime}
is the union of some coordinate hyperplanes,
such that
X^{\prime}\cap S^{\circ}
is mapped to
\{\mathbf{0}_{3}\}\times(\mathbb{C}^{n}\setminus H^{\prime})
.
The morphism
\nu^{\prime}
is induced by taking the fiber product of
W^{\prime}_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}\cap W^{\circ}
over the canonical finite morphism
Z^{\prime}\rightarrow Y_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}\times(\mathbb{C}^{n}\setminus H^{\prime})
as above.

We will divide the proof of the proposition into a sequence of lemmas.
The sketch is as follows.
As explained in the previous paragraph, we first construct an open embedding
X^{\circ}\rightarrow Y\times\check{S}^{\circ}
.
We may construct a finite morphism
\overline{X}\rightarrow X
by taking basechange as in the previous paragraph.
However, since we have little information on the fundamental group of
X^{\circ}_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}
,
it is not clear to us that
\overline{X}
satisfies the item (4) and (5) of the proposition,
which signify that
\overline{X}
is independent of the open embedding
X^{\circ}\rightarrow Y\times\check{S}^{\circ}
.
To deal with this problem,
we will construct explicitly a tubular neighborhood
V
of
S^{\circ}
in
X^{\circ}
,
such that
\pi_{1}(V_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}})\cong\pi_{1}(Y_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}})\times\mathbb{Z}^{k}\cong\pi_{1}(Y_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}\times\check{S}^{\circ})
.
Then the finite cover
\overline{V}\rightarrow V
constructed by this method
is independent of the choice of the open embedding from
X^{\circ}
to
Y\times\check{S}^{\circ}
.
A new issue is that,
V
may not extend to an open neighborhood of
S
in
X
.
It can collapse when approaching to
H\subseteq S
.
Thus
\overline{V}
may not extend to a covering space over any neighborhood of
S
.
Our solution to this is to blow up
X
at the
H_{i}
’s as in Subsection
3.C
.
We show that, after some blowups, the preimage of
V
extends to some open neighborhood
W
of the strict transform of
S
.
Then the covering space
\overline{V}
extends to a finite cover
\overline{W}\rightarrow W
.

Lemma 6.8
.

With the notation in Proposition
6.7
,
there is an open embedding, denoted by
\varphi^{-1}
, from
M^{\circ}
to
Y\times\check{S}^{\circ}
,
whose inverse
\varphi
can be written in coordinates as

\varphi\colon(\overline{x},\overline{y},\overline{z},s)\mapstochar\rightarrow(a(s)\overline{x},b(s)\overline{y},\zeta(\overline{y},\overline{z},s),s)=(x,y,z,s),

where
a(s),b(s)
are holomorphic functions on
\check{S}
, and

\zeta(\overline{y},\overline{z},s)=\frac{1}{\gamma(s)}\Big(c(s)\overline{z}-u(b(s)\overline{y},s)\cdot b(s)\overline{y}\Big),

for some holomorphic function
c(s)
on
\check{S}
.
Moreover, we can impose that the zero loci of
a,b,c
are contained in
H
,
and that
b,c
are divisible by
\gamma
.

Proof.

We treat the case when

F(x,y,z,s)=x^{2}+\alpha(s)y^{3}+\beta(s)\cdot y(\gamma(s)z+u(y,s)y)^{3}.

The other cases are similar.
In order that
\varphi
is formally well-defined, it is sufficient that, there is a unit function
v(s)
on
\check{S}^{\circ}
, such that

(6.1)

a^{2}\cdot\overline{x}^{2}+b^{3}\alpha\cdot\overline{y}^{3}+\beta b\cdot\overline{y}(\gamma\cdot\zeta+u(b\overline{y},s)\cdot b\overline{y})^{3}=v\cdot(\overline{x}^{2}+\overline{y}^{3}+\overline{y}\cdot\overline{z}^{3}).

The LHS above is equal to

a^{2}\cdot\overline{x}^{2}+\alpha\cdot b^{3}\cdot\overline{y}^{3}+\beta\cdot bc^{3}\cdot\overline{y}\cdot\overline{z}^{3}.

Hence we need to solve

a^{2}=\alpha\cdot b^{3}=\beta\cdot bc^{3}=v.

Since the zero loci of
\alpha
and
\beta
are contained in
H
, we can write

\alpha=T_{1}^{l_{1}}\cdots T_{k}^{l_{k}}\cdot\alpha^{\prime}\mbox{ and }\beta=T_{1}^{l^{\prime}_{1}}\cdots T_{k}^{l^{\prime}_{k}}\cdot\beta^{\prime}

where
l_{1},...,l_{k},l_{1}^{\prime},...,l_{k}^{\prime}\geq 0
are integers divisible by
6
after the item (2) of Setup
6.6
,
and
\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}
are unit holomorphic functions on
\check{S}
.
Since
\check{S}
is simply connected, we can take the logarithms of
\alpha^{\prime}
and
\beta^{\prime}
.
Then we deduce that
a,b,c,v
exist as meromorphic functions on
\check{S}
, with poles and zeros contained in
H
.
For example, we can set
b=1
,
v=\alpha
,

a=T_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}\cdot l_{1}}\cdots T_{k}^{\frac{1}{2}\cdot l_{k}}\cdot(\alpha^{\prime})^{\frac{1}{2}}\mbox{ and }c=T_{1}^{\frac{1}{3}(l_{1}-l^{\prime}_{1})}\cdots T_{k}^{\frac{1}{3}(l_{k}-l^{\prime}_{k})}\cdot(\alpha^{\prime})^{\frac{1}{3}}(\beta^{\prime})^{-\frac{1}{3}}.

We also note that, if
(a,b,c,v)
satisfies (
6.1
), then so does
(T^{9}\cdot a,T^{6}\cdot b,T^{4}\cdot c,T^{18}\cdot v)
, see also Lemma
6.3
.
Since the zero locus of
\gamma
is contained in
H
,
up to multiplying
a,b,c
by powers of
T
,
we may assume that they are holomorphic functions and that
b,c
are divisible by
\gamma
.

The morphism
\varphi^{-1}
from
M^{\circ}
to
Y\times\check{S}^{\circ}
can be written in coordinates as

\varphi^{-1}\colon(x,y,z,s)\mapstochar\rightarrow(a(s)^{-1}x,b(s)^{-1}y,c(s)^{-1}(\gamma(s)z+u(y,s)y),s)=(\overline{x},\overline{y},\overline{z},s).

Then
\varphi^{-1}
is a well-defined morphism on
M^{\circ}
.
Since
a,b,c,\gamma
are nowhere vanishing holomorphic functions on
\check{S}^{\circ}
, we see that
\varphi^{-1}
is locally biholomorphic
and is injective.
Hence
\varphi^{-1}
is an open embedding.
∎

We remark that the divisibility condition in the item (2) of Setup
6.6
is to guarantee the existence of
a,b,c
in the previous lemma.

Lemma 6.9
.

We fix a morphism
\varphi
as in Lemma
6.8
so that
\varphi^{-1}
is an open embedding from
M^{\circ}
to
Y\times\check{S}^{\circ}
.
Then there are integers
p,q,r>0
such that the endomorphism
\eta
of
Y\times\check{S}
defined by

\eta\colon(\overline{x},\overline{y},\overline{z},s)\mapstochar\rightarrow(T^{p}\overline{x},T^{q}\overline{y},T^{r}\overline{z},s),

satisfies the following properties.
The image
\eta(U\times S^{\circ})
is contained in
\varphi^{-1}(M^{\prime}\cap M^{\circ})
.
In other words, we have

(\varphi\circ\eta)(U\times S^{\circ})\subseteq M^{\prime}\cap M^{\circ}\subseteq X^{\circ}.

Furthermore, for any point
s\in S\cap H
and any neighborhood
X^{\prime}
of
s
in
X
,
there is an open neighborhood
S^{\prime}
of
s
in
S
,
such that

(\varphi\circ\eta)\big(U\times(S^{\prime}\cap S^{\circ})\big)

is contained in
X^{\prime}\cap X^{\circ}
.

Proof.

Since
a
,
b
,
c\cdot\gamma^{-1}
and
b\cdot\gamma^{-1}
are holomorphic functions on
\check{S}
,
they are bounded on
S
.
Hence there is a positive number
K
such that, for any
s\in S
and any
(\overline{x},\overline{y},\overline{z})\in\mathbb{C}^{3}
,

|a(s)\overline{x}|\leq K|\overline{x}|,\ |b(s)\overline{y}|\leq K|\overline{y}|\mbox{ and }|\frac{c(s)}{\gamma(s)}\overline{z}|\leq K|\overline{z}|.

Since
u
is a holomorphic function on
\mathbb{D}\times\check{S}
, we may assume that
|u(b(s)\overline{y},s)|
is bounded for
(\overline{y},s)\in\mathbb{D}_{\varepsilon}\times S
, where
\mathbb{D}_{\varepsilon}\subseteq\mathbb{C}
is the disc of some radius
\varepsilon>0
.
Hence we may assume further that

|\zeta(\overline{y},\overline{z},s)|=\Big|\frac{c(s)}{\gamma(s)}\overline{z}-\frac{b(s)}{\gamma(s)}\cdot u(b(s)\overline{y},s)\cdot\overline{y}\Big|\leq K(|\overline{z}|+|\overline{y}|)

whenever
|\overline{y}|<\varepsilon
and
s\in S
.

Since
M^{\prime}\cap M^{\circ}
is a hypersurface defined in
\mathbb{D^{\prime}}^{3}\times S^{\circ}
, in order that a point
(\overline{x},\overline{y},\overline{z},s)\in Y\times S^{\circ}
is contained in
\varphi^{-1}(M^{\prime}\cap M^{\circ})
, it is necessary and sufficient that

|a(s)\overline{x}|<R^{\prime},\ |b(s)\overline{y}|<R^{\prime}\mbox{ and }|\zeta(\overline{y},\overline{z},s)|<R^{\prime},

where
R^{\prime}
is the radius of
\mathbb{D}^{\prime}
.
Since
S
in contained in a polydisc of radius smaller than 1,
we have
|T|<1
.
Since
U
is bounded, we deduce that there is some integer
{N_{0}}>0
such that

K|T^{N_{0}}\overline{x}|<R^{\prime},\ K|T^{N_{0}}\overline{y}|<R^{\prime},\ |T^{N_{0}}\overline{y}|<\varepsilon,\ \mbox{ and }K(|T^{N_{0}}\overline{y}|+|T^{N_{0}}\overline{z}|)<R^{\prime}.

for all
(\overline{x},\overline{y},\overline{z},s)\subseteq U\times S^{\circ}
.
By Lemma
6.3
, there exist integers
p,q,r\geq{N_{0}}
such that

\eta\colon(\overline{x},\overline{y},\overline{z},s)\mapstochar\rightarrow(T^{p}\overline{x},T^{q}\overline{y},T^{r}\overline{z},s),

is a well-defined endomorphism of
Y\times S
.
We then deduce that
\eta(U\times S^{\circ})
is contained in
\varphi^{-1}(M^{\prime}\cap M^{\circ})
.

For the second property on
\eta
, we assume that
X^{\prime}
is a neighborhood of
s
in
X
.
Then there is a positive number
\varepsilon^{\prime}
,
there is an open neighborhood
S^{\prime}\subseteq S
of
s
in
S
,
such that

M\cap\big((\mathbb{D}_{\varepsilon^{\prime}})^{3}\times S^{\prime}\big)\subseteq X^{\prime},

where
\mathbb{D}_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}\subseteq\mathbb{C}
is the disc of radius
\varepsilon^{\prime}
.
We remark that the value of
T
at
s
is equal to 0.
Hence we may pick
S^{\prime}
in order that
|T|^{N_{0}}
is very small on
S^{\prime}
.
More precisely, since
U
is bounded, we can pick
S^{\prime}
so that

K|T^{p}\overline{x}|<\varepsilon^{\prime},\ K|T^{q}\overline{y}|<\varepsilon^{\prime},\ |T^{q}\overline{y}|<\varepsilon\mbox{ and }K(|T^{q}\overline{y}|+|T^{r}\overline{z}|)<\varepsilon^{\prime}.

for all
(\overline{x},\overline{y},\overline{z},s)\in U\times S^{\prime}
.
It follows that

(\varphi\circ\eta)\big(U\times(S^{\prime}\cap S^{\circ})\big)

is contained in
X^{\prime}
.
This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

In the remainder of the section, by replacing
\varphi
with
\varphi\circ\eta
,
we assume that
\varphi
satisfies the properties of
\varphi\circ\eta
in Lemma
6.9
.
Let

V=\varphi(U\times S^{\circ})\subseteq M^{\prime}\cap M^{\circ}\subseteq X^{\circ}.

Then
V
is a tubular neighborhood of
S^{\circ}
in
X^{\circ}
.
The fundamental group
\pi_{1}(V_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}})
is isomorphic to
\pi_{1}(Y_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}})\times\mathbb{Z}^{k}
.
We observe that
V
does not extend to an open neighborhood of
S
in
X
.
Indeed, since
N_{0}>0
, a point
(x,y,z,s)\in V
approaches to
(0,0,0,s_{H})
when
s
tends to a point
s_{H}\in H
.
In the following lemma, we show that, if we blowup
X
at the
H_{i}
’s for sufficiently many times,
then the preimage of
V
extends to a neighborhood of the strict transform of
S
.

Lemma 6.10
.

With the notation above,
there exists an integer
N>0
such that the following property holds.
Let
\rho\colon\widehat{M}\rightarrow M
be a morphism obtained by blowing up
M
at the
H_{i}^{\prime}
s as in Subsection
3.C
,
such that there are at least
N
blowups whose centers are
H_{i}
for all
i
.
Let
\widehat{X}=\rho^{-1}(X)
,
\widehat{X}^{\circ}=\rho^{-1}(X^{\circ})
,
\widehat{V}=\rho^{-1}(V)
and let
\widehat{S}
be the strict transform of
S
in
\widehat{M}
.
Then there is an open neighborhoods
W
of
\widehat{S}
in
\widehat{X}
,
such that
W^{\circ}:=W\cap\widehat{X}^{\circ}
is contained in
\widehat{V}
,
and that
W
is invariant under the natural action of
G
on
\widehat{X}
.
Moreover, we have
\rho(W\cap\rho^{-1}(\Delta))\subseteq H\cap S
.

Proof.

There is some
\varepsilon>0
such that the intersection
Y\cap(\mathbb{D}_{\varepsilon})^{3}
is contained in
U
.
Then
\varphi((Y\cap(\mathbb{D}_{\varepsilon})^{3})\times S^{\circ})\subseteq V
.
The application
\varphi^{-1}\colon M^{\circ}\rightarrow Y\times S^{\circ}
can be written with coordinates as

\varphi^{-1}\colon(x,y,z,s)\mapstochar\rightarrow(a(s)^{-1}x,b(s)^{-1}y,\xi(y,z,s),s),

where
\xi(y,z,s)=c(s)^{-1}(\gamma(s)z+u(y,s)y)
.
Hence, a point
(x,y,z,s)\in M^{\circ}
with
s\in S^{\circ}
is contained in

\varphi\big((Y\cap(\mathbb{D}_{\varepsilon})^{3})\times S^{\circ}\big)

if and only if

(6.2)

|a(s)^{-1}x|<\varepsilon,\ |b(s)^{-1}y|<\varepsilon\mbox{ and }|c(s)^{-1}(\gamma(s)z+u(y,s)y)|<\varepsilon.

Since the zero loci of
a,b,c
are contained in
H
,
there is some positive integer
N
such that

T^{N}\cdot a(s)^{-1},\ T^{N}\cdot b(s)^{-1}\mbox{ and }T^{N}\cdot c(s)^{-1}

are holomorphic functions on
\check{S}
.
We will show that this number
N
satisfies the assertion of the lemma.

As shown in Subsection
3.C
,
there is a coordinates system
(\hat{x},\hat{y},\hat{z},s)
on a neighborhood of the closure of
\widehat{S}
in
\widehat{M}
,
such that

\rho
can be expressed as

\rho\colon(\hat{x},\hat{y},\hat{z},s)\mapstochar\rightarrow(\hat{T}\hat{x},\hat{T}\hat{y},\hat{T}\hat{z},s),

where
\hat{T}=T_{1}^{d_{1}}\cdots T_{k}^{d_{k}}
and
d_{i}
is the number of blowups in
\rho
whose centers are
H_{i}
.
By pulling back the conditions of (
6.2
) to
\widehat{M}
, we obtain the following conditions

|\hat{T}\cdot a(s)^{-1}\hat{x}|<\varepsilon,\ |\hat{T}\cdot b(s)^{-1}\hat{y}|<\varepsilon\mbox{ and }|\hat{T}\cdot c(s)^{-1}(\gamma(s)\hat{z}+u(\hat{T}\hat{y},s)\hat{y})|<\varepsilon.

Since
d_{1},...,d_{k}\geq N
, the functions

\hat{T}\cdot a(s)^{-1},\ \hat{T}\cdot b(s)^{-1}\mbox{ and }\hat{T}\cdot c(s)^{-1}

are holomorphic functions on
\check{S}
.
Hence they are bounded on
S
.
Therefore there is some
\delta>0
such that if

\widehat{M}^{\circ}:=\rho^{-1}(M^{\circ})
and
if
\hat{o}=(\hat{x},\hat{y},\hat{z},s)
is contained in

\widehat{M}^{\circ}\cap((\mathbb{D}_{\delta})^{3}\times\widehat{S})
,
then

\rho(\hat{o})\in\varphi\big((Y\cap(\mathbb{D}_{\varepsilon})^{3})\times S^{\circ}\big).

Let
\widehat{W}=((\mathbb{D}_{\delta})^{3}\times\widehat{S})\cap\widehat{M}
.
It follows that

\rho(\widehat{W}\cap\widehat{M}^{\circ})\subseteq V.

Thus we get
\widehat{W}\cap\widehat{M}^{\circ}\subseteq\widehat{V}\subseteq\widehat{X}
.

Assume that
\hat{o}\in\widehat{W}\cap\rho^{-1}(\Delta)=\widehat{W}\setminus\widehat{M}^{\circ}
is a point with coordinate
(\hat{x},\hat{y},\hat{z},s)
in
(\mathbb{D}_{\delta})^{3}\times\widehat{S}
.
Then
\hat{T}(s)=0
and
\rho(\hat{o})
has coordinates
(0,0,0,s)
in
M
.
This implies that
\rho(\hat{o})\in H\cap S\subseteq X
.
In particular, we see that
\widehat{W}\subseteq\widehat{X}
.
Hence we can define
W=\bigcap_{g\in G}g(\widehat{W})
.
Then
W
is a
G
-invariant neighborhood of
\widehat{S}
in
\widehat{X}
.
This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

The following lemma is the key to the item (4) of Proposition
6.7
.

Lemma 6.11
.

With the notation in Lemma
6.10
,
we fix such a bimeromorphic morphism
\rho\colon\widehat{X}\rightarrow X
such that there are
6N^{\prime}
blowups whose centers are
H_{i}
for all
i
, where
N^{\prime}\geq N
is an integer.
Then there is a sequence of morphisms of fundamental groups

\textstyle{\pi_{1}(W^{\circ}_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{*}}
\textstyle{\pi_{1}(V_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi^{-1}_{*}}
\textstyle{\pi_{1}(Y_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}\times\check{S}^{\circ}).}

We denote by
\iota\colon\pi_{1}(W^{\circ}_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}})\rightarrow\pi_{1}(Y_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}\times\check{S}^{\circ})
the composition of the previous sequence.
Then
\iota
is surjective.

Furthermore, if
\psi\colon X^{\circ}\rightarrow Y\times\check{S}^{\circ}
is an open embedding,
whose restriction on
\check{S}^{\circ}\subseteq X^{\circ}
is equal to the identity map from
\check{S}^{\circ}
to
\{\mathbf{0}_{3}\}\times\check{S}^{\circ}
.
Then the morphism of fundamental groups

(\psi\circ\rho)_{*}\colon\pi_{1}(W^{\circ}_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}})\rightarrow\pi_{1}(Y_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}\times\check{S}^{\circ})

is surjective and its kernel is equal to the kernel of
\iota
.

Proof.

We notice that
\widehat{S}\cong S
.
From the construction of
W
in Lemma
6.10
,
we see that if
\widehat{H}
is the preimage of
H
in
\widehat{S}
,
then the data
W
,
\rho^{-1}(\Delta)|_{W}
,
\widehat{S}
,
\widehat{H}
and
G
satisfy the items (1) and (2) of Setup
6.6
.
If
o_{\widehat{S}}
is the preimage of
o_{S}
in
\widehat{S}
,
then there is an open neighborhood
S_{1}
of
o_{\widehat{S}}
in
\widehat{S}
,
which plays the same role for
o_{\widehat{S}}
as
S
for
o_{S}
in the item (4) of Setup
6.6
.
By applying Lemma
6.9
to
W
,
there is an open embedding
\hat{\varphi}\colon U\times S_{1}^{\circ}\rightarrow W
whose restriction on
\{\mathbf{0}_{3}\}\times S_{1}^{\circ}
is the identity map to
S_{1}^{\circ}
,
where
S_{1}^{\circ}=S_{1}\setminus\widehat{H}
.
Since there are natural isomorphisms

\pi_{1}(S_{1}^{\circ})\cong\pi_{1}(S^{\circ})\cong\pi_{1}(\check{S}^{\circ})\cong\mathbb{Z}^{k},

by Lemma
6.4
,
the composition of the following sequence

\textstyle{\pi_{1}(U_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}\times S_{1}^{\circ})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hat{\varphi}_{*}}
\textstyle{\pi_{1}(W^{\circ}_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota}
\textstyle{\pi_{1}(Y_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}\times\check{S}^{\circ})}

is an isomorphism.
Thus
\iota
is surjective.

We recall that
\varphi\colon U\times S^{\circ}\rightarrow V
is an isomorphism.
Since there is a natural isomorphism
\pi_{1}(U_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}})\cong\pi_{1}(Y_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}})
, we deduce that

\varphi^{-1}_{*}\colon\pi_{1}(V_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}})\rightarrow\pi_{1}(Y_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}\times\check{S}^{\circ})

is an isomorphism,
and we obtain that
\rho_{*}\colon\pi_{1}(W^{\circ}_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}})\rightarrow\pi_{1}(V_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}})
is surjective.
Moreover, the kernel of
\iota
is equal to the one of
\rho_{*}
.

Assume that we have a morphism
\psi\colon X^{\circ}\rightarrow Y\times\check{S}^{\circ}
as in the second part of the lemma.
Then the morphism

\psi\circ\varphi\colon U\times S^{\circ}\rightarrow Y\times\check{S}^{\circ}

is an open embedding whose restriction on
\{\mathbf{0}_{3}\}\times S^{\circ}
is the identity map.
Thus it induces an isomorphism from
\pi_{1}(U_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}\times S^{\circ})
to
\pi_{1}(Y_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}\times\check{S}^{\circ})
by Lemma
6.4
.
Hence

\psi_{*}\colon\pi_{1}(V_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}})\rightarrow\pi_{1}(Y_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}\times\check{S}^{\circ})

is an isomorphism.
Since
\rho_{*}\colon\pi_{1}(W^{\circ}_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}})\rightarrow\pi_{1}(V_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}})
is surjective,
we deduce that
(\psi\circ\rho)_{*}
is surjective.
Furthermore, the kernel of
(\psi\circ\rho)_{*}
is the one of
\rho_{*}
,
which is also the kernel of
\iota
.
This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

The item (5) of Proposition
6.7
follows essentially from the next lemma.

Lemma 6.12
.

With the notation in Lemma
6.11
,
let
\hat{o}\in W\setminus\widehat{X}^{\circ}_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}
be a point and let
o=\rho(\hat{o})\in X
.
Let
\Sigma\subseteq X
be an open neighborhood of
o
, and let
\Sigma^{\circ}=\Sigma\cap X^{\circ}
.
Assume that for
i=1,2
, there is an open embedding

\psi_{i}\colon\Sigma^{\circ}\rightarrow Y\times(\mathbb{C}^{n}\setminus H^{i})

satisfying the following properties.
Each
H^{i}
is the union of some coordinate hyperplanes in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
,
the restriction
\psi_{i}|_{\check{S}\cap\Sigma^{\circ}}
has images contained in
\{\mathbf{0}_{3}\}\times\mathbb{C}^{n}
,
and it extends to an open embedding from
\check{S}\cap\Sigma
to
\mathbb{C}^{n}
such that
\psi_{i}(\check{S}\cap\Sigma^{\circ})=\psi_{i}(\check{S}\cap\Sigma)\setminus H^{i}
.

Then there is an open neighborhood
\widehat{\Sigma}\subseteq W
of
\hat{o}
, such that
the following properties hold.

(1)

The morphisms of fundamental groups from
\pi_{1}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}\cap\widehat{X}^{\circ})
to
\pi_{1}(Y_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}\times(\mathbb{C}^{n}\setminus H^{i}))

induced by
\psi_{i}\circ\rho
, with
i=1,2
, have the same kernel.

(2)

Let
k^{1}
and
k^{2}
be the numbers of the irreducible components of
H^{1}
and
H^{2}
respectively,
and let
k^{\prime}
be the number of the irreducible components of
H
passing through
o
.
For
i=1,2
, the image of
(\psi_{i}\circ\rho)_{*}
is the conjugacy class of a normal subgroup and

\pi_{1}(Y_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}\times(\mathbb{C}^{n}\setminus H^{i}))/(\psi_{i}\circ\rho)_{*}(\pi_{1}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}\cap\widehat{X}^{\circ}))\cong\mathbb{Z}^{k^{i}-k^{\prime}}.

Proof.

We first assume that the point
\hat{o}
is in
W^{\circ}
.
Then
\hat{o}\in W^{\circ}_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sing}}}
.
Since
\rho
is an isomorphism on
W^{\circ}
,
we deduce that
o\in S^{\circ}\subseteq X^{\circ}
.
It follows that
k^{\prime}=0
.
We note that
X
has the same type of singularities at points of
S^{\circ}
.
Hence there is a contractible open neighborhood
S^{\prime}
of
o
in
S^{\circ}
,
an open neighborhood
V^{\prime}
of
o
in
\Sigma^{\circ}
,
and an open neighborhood
Y^{\prime}
of
\mathbf{0}_{3}
in
Y
,
such that
V^{\prime}\cong Y^{\prime}\times S^{\prime}
.
We may assume further that the natural morphism
\pi_{1}(Y^{\prime}_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}})\rightarrow\pi_{1}(Y_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}})
is isomorphic.
Let
\widehat{\Sigma}
be the preimage of
V^{\prime}
in
\widehat{X}
.
Then we have
\hat{o}\in\widehat{\Sigma}\subseteq\widehat{X}^{\circ}
and
\rho
induces an isomorphism from
\widehat{\Sigma}
to
V^{\prime}
.
In particular,
\rho(\widehat{\Sigma})\cap\check{S}^{\circ}=S^{\prime}
, and there are natural isomorphisms

\pi_{1}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}})\cong\pi_{1}(V^{\prime}_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}})\cong\pi_{1}(Y_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}).

Since
\psi_{i}
induces open embeddings from
V^{\prime}\cong Y^{\prime}\times S^{\prime}
to
Y\times(\mathbb{C}^{n}\setminus H^{i})
, whose restriction on
S^{\prime}=V^{\prime}_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sing}}}
has images contained in
\{\mathbf{0}_{3}\}\times(\mathbb{C}^{n}\setminus H^{i})
,
by Lemma
6.4
, it induces an injective morphism from
\pi_{1}(V^{\prime}_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}})

to
\pi_{1}(Y_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}\times(\mathbb{C}^{n}\setminus H^{i}))
.
Therefore, the morphism
from
\pi_{1}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}\cap\widehat{X}^{\circ})
to
\pi_{1}(Y_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}\times(\mathbb{C}^{n}\setminus H^{i}))

induced by
\psi_{i}\circ\rho
is injective.
This shows the item (1).
Lemma
6.4
also implies that
the image of
(\psi_{i}\circ\rho)_{*}
is the image of
\pi_{1}(Y_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}\times\psi_{i}(S^{\prime}))
induced by the natural inclusion.
This proves the item (2).

It remains to treat the case when
\hat{o}\in W\setminus W^{\circ}=W\cap\rho^{-1}(\Delta)
.
This assumption implies that
o\in H\cap S
by Lemma
6.10
.
We recall that
\theta\colon M\rightarrow\check{S}
is the projection sending a point
(x,y,z,s)\in M
to
s\in\check{S}
.
Let
\hat{\theta}\colon\widehat{M}\rightarrow\check{S}
be the composition
\theta\circ\rho
.
By Lemma
6.9
, there is an open neighborhood
S^{\prime}
of
o
in
S
, such that

V^{\prime}:=\varphi\big(U\times(S^{\prime}\cap S^{\circ})\big)=V\cap\theta^{-1}(S^{\prime})

is contained in
\Sigma^{\circ}
.
Up to shrinking
S^{\prime}
, we can assume that it is isomorphic to a polydisc whose origin is
o
,
and
H|_{S^{\prime}}
is isomorphic to the union of coordinates hyperplanes.
We define
\widehat{\Sigma}=W\cap\hat{\theta}^{-1}(S^{\prime})
.
Then
\hat{o}\in\widehat{\Sigma}
,

\rho(\widehat{\Sigma})\cap\check{S}=S^{\prime},\ \rho(\widehat{\Sigma}\cap\widehat{X}^{\circ})\subseteq V^{\prime}\mbox{ and }\rho(\widehat{\Sigma}_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}\cap\widehat{X}^{\circ})\subseteq V^{\prime}_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}.

We notice that
V^{\prime}\cong U\times(S^{\prime}\cap\check{S}^{\circ})
.
By assumption, for
i=1,2
,

\psi_{i}
induces open an embedding
from
V^{\prime}
to
Y\times(\mathbb{C}^{n}\setminus H^{i})
,
whose restriction on
S^{\prime}\cap\check{S}^{\circ}=V^{\prime}_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sing}}}
has image contained in
\{\mathbf{0}_{3}\}\times\mathbb{C}^{n}
,
and extends to an open embedding from
S^{\prime}
to
\mathbb{C}^{n}
,
such that
\psi_{i}(S^{\prime}\cap\check{S}^{\circ})=\psi_{i}(S^{\prime})\setminus H^{i}
.
Hence by Lemma
6.4
,
the morphism

\pi_{1}(V^{\prime}_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}})\rightarrow\pi_{1}(Y_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}\times(\mathbb{C}^{n}\setminus H^{i}))

induced by
\psi_{i}
is injective.
We then deduce that the kernel of the morphism
from
\pi_{1}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}\cap\widehat{X}^{\circ})
to
\pi_{1}(Y_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}\times(\mathbb{C}^{n}\setminus H^{i}))

induced by
\psi_{i}\circ\rho
is the same as the one of

\rho_{*}\colon\pi_{1}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}\cap\widehat{X}^{\circ})\rightarrow\pi_{1}(V^{\prime}_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}})
,
which is independent of
i
.
Hence we obtain the item (1).

Lemma
6.4
also implies that the image of
(\psi_{i})_{*}(\pi_{1}(V^{\prime}_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}))
is the same as the image of
\pi_{1}(Y_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}\times\psi_{i}(S^{\prime}\cap\check{S}^{\circ}))
induced by the natural inclusion.
Furthermore, by the same argument as in the first two paragraph of the proof of Lemma
6.11
, we see that the morphism
\rho_{*}\colon\pi_{1}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}\cap\widehat{X}^{\circ})\rightarrow\pi_{1}(V^{\prime}_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}})
is surjective.
Hence the item (2) holds as well.
This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Now we can complete the proof of Proposition
6.7
.

Proof of Proposition
6.7
.

We let
N
be the integer in Lemma
6.10
and we fix an integer
N^{\prime}\geq N
.
With the notation in Lemma
6.11
, there is a morphism of fundamental groups

\iota=(\varphi^{-1}\circ\rho)_{*}\colon\pi_{1}(W^{\circ}_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}})\rightarrow\pi_{1}(Y_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}\times\check{S}^{\circ}).

We note that
\pi_{1}(Y_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}\times\check{S}^{\circ})\cong G_{Y}\times\mathbb{Z}^{k}
, where
G_{Y}
is the fundamental group of
Y_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}
, which is finite.
Let
\mathcal{N}\subseteq\pi_{1}(Y_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}\times\check{S}^{\circ})
be the normal subgroup defined in Lemma
6.5
,
and let
\mathcal{H}=\iota^{-1}(\mathcal{N})
.
Then
\mathcal{H}
is a normal subgroup of
\pi_{1}(W^{\circ}_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}})
, and it determines a finite Galois étale cover of
W^{\circ}_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}
.
Let
\nu\colon\overline{W}\rightarrow W
be the induced finite cover with
\overline{W}
normal, see Theorem
2.4
.
Then
\nu
is Galois and quasi-étale over
W^{\circ}
.
We have hence proved the items (1) and (2).

We will show that
\overline{W}^{\circ}:=\nu^{-1}(W^{\circ})
is smooth.
Let
Z\rightarrow Y\times\check{S}^{\circ}
be the finite quasi-étale morphism induced by the subgroup
\mathcal{N}\subseteq\pi_{1}(Y_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}\times\check{S}^{\circ})
, see Theorem
2.4
.
Then it factors through
\overline{Y}\times\check{S}^{\circ}\rightarrow Y\times\check{S}^{\circ}
, where
\overline{Y}\rightarrow Y
is the quasi-étale cover induced by the universal cover of
Y_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}
.
In particular,
\overline{Y}
is smooth.
The Zariski’s purity theorem then implies that
Z
is smooth.
We note that

\nu|_{\overline{W}^{\circ}}
is the basechange of
\varphi^{-1}\circ\rho\colon W^{\circ}\rightarrow Y\times\check{S}^{\circ}

over
Z\rightarrow Y\times\check{S}^{\circ}
.

\textstyle{\overline{W}^{\circ}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\nu}
\textstyle{W^{\circ}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\overline{Y}\times\check{S}^{\circ}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y\times\check{S}^{\circ}}

Since
\rho
is an isomorphism on
W^{\circ}
, we see that

\varphi^{-1}\circ\rho
is an open embedding on
W^{\circ}
.
Hence
\overline{W}^{\circ}
is embedded into
Z
as an open subset, and is smooth.
This proves the item (3).

To prove the item (4), it is enough to show that
\mathcal{H}
is
G
-invariant.
Let
g\in G
be an element.
Then
g^{-1}(\mathcal{H})
is the preiamge of
\mathcal{H}
under the morphism of fundamental groups induced by
g\colon W\rightarrow W
.
Let

\iota^{\prime}:=(g^{-1}\circ\varphi^{-1}\circ\rho\circ g)_{*}\colon\pi_{1}(W^{\circ}_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}})\rightarrow\pi_{1}(Y_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}\times\check{S}^{\circ}),

where the morphism
g^{-1}
above is the automorphism of
Y\times\check{S}^{\circ}
defined by the action of
G
on
\check{S}^{\circ}
.
Then we have

g^{-1}(\mathcal{H})=((\varphi^{-1}\circ\rho\circ g)_{*})^{-1}(\mathcal{N})=(\iota^{\prime})^{-1}(\mathcal{N}).

We note that
\rho\circ g=g\circ\rho
as morphisms from
W
to
X
.
By applying the second part of Lemma
6.11
with

\psi=g^{-1}\circ\varphi^{-1}\circ g\colon X^{\circ}\rightarrow Y\times\check{S}^{\circ},

we deduce that the two morphisms
\iota
and
\iota^{\prime}
are surjective of the same kernel.
Hence, there is an automorphism
\tau
of
\pi_{1}(Y_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}\times\check{S}^{\circ})
such that

\iota=\tau\circ\iota^{\prime}.

By Lemma
6.5
, we have
\tau^{-1}(\mathcal{N})=\mathcal{N}
.
It follows that
g^{-1}(\mathcal{H})=\mathcal{H}
.
This shows the item (4).

It remains to prove the item (5).
If
\hat{o}\in\widehat{X}^{\circ}_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}
,
then by construction,
\nu
an
\nu^{\prime}
are both étale over
\hat{o}
.
We can let
W^{\prime\prime}
be a smooth contractible neighborhood of
\hat{o}
in
W^{\prime}
in this case.

Next we assume that
\hat{o}
is not contained in
\widehat{X}^{\circ}_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}
.
Then
o=\rho(\hat{o})\in S
, see Lemma
6.10
.
As explained in the paragraph after Proposition
6.7
,
by assumption,
there is an open neighborhood
X^{\prime}
of
o=\rho(\hat{o})
containing
\rho(W^{\prime})
,
there is an open embedding
\psi\colon(X^{\prime}\cap X^{\circ})\rightarrow Y\times(\mathbb{C}^{n}\setminus H^{\prime})
, where
H^{\prime}
is the union of some coordinate hyperplanes in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
, such that the following properties hold.
The restriction of
\psi
on
X^{\prime}\cap\check{S}^{\circ}

has images contained in
\{\mathbf{0}_{3}\}\times\mathbb{C}^{n}
.
It extends to an open embedding from
X^{\prime}\cap\check{S}
to
\mathbb{C}^{n}
,
such that
\psi(X^{\prime}\cap\check{S}^{\circ})=\psi(X^{\prime}\cap\check{S})\setminus H^{\prime}
.
Furthermore,

\nu^{\prime}
is induced by the kernel of the composition of the following (conjugacy classes of) morphisms of groups

\textstyle{\pi_{1}((W^{\prime}\cap\widehat{X}^{\circ})_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{*}}
\textstyle{\pi_{1}((X^{\prime}\cap X^{\circ})_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}})}
\scriptstyle{\psi_{*}}
\textstyle{\pi_{1}(Y_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}\times(\mathbb{C}^{n}\setminus H^{\prime}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\pi_{1}(Y_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}\times(\mathbb{C}^{n}\setminus H^{\prime}))/\mathcal{N}^{\prime},}

where
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
is the canonical normal subgroup of
\pi_{1}(Y_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}\times(\mathbb{C}^{n}\setminus H^{\prime}))
defined in Lemma
6.5
.

By Lemma
6.12
and its proof,
there is a contractible open neighborhood
S^{\prime\prime}
of
o
in
S
,
and an open neighborhood

of

in

,
such that
\rho(W^{\prime\prime})\cap\check{S}=S^{\prime\prime}
,
and that the two morphisms

\sigma_{1}=(\varphi^{-1}\circ\rho)_{*}\colon\pi_{1}((W^{\prime\prime}\cap\widehat{X}^{\circ})_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}})\rightarrow\pi_{1}(Y_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}\times\check{S}^{\circ})

and

\sigma_{2}=(\psi\circ\rho)_{*}\colon\pi_{1}((W^{\prime\prime}\cap\widehat{X}^{\circ})_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}})\rightarrow\pi_{1}(Y_{\operatorname{\textsubscript{\rm sm}}}\times(\mathbb{C}^{n}\setminus H^{\prime}))

have the same kernel.
Their images are both normal subgroups and both isomorphic to

,
where

is the number of the components of

which pass through
